A Novel Theoretical Analysis for Clustering Heteroscedastic Gaussian Data without Knowledge of the Number of Clusters
Dominique Pastor dominique.pastor@imt-atlantique.fr, Elsa Dupraz elsa.dupraz@imt-atlantique.fr, Ismail Hbilou ismail.hbilou@imt-atlantique.net, Guillaume Ansel guillaume.ansel@optisea.fr

Abstract
This paper addresses the problem of clustering measurement vectors that are heteroscedastic in that they can have different covariance matrices. From the assumption that the measurement vectors within a given cluster are Gaussian distributed with possibly different and unknown covariant matrices around the cluster centroid, we introduce a novel cost function to estimate the centroids. The zeros of the gradient of this cost function turn out to be the fixed-points of a certain function. As such, the approach generalizes the methodology employed to derive the existing Mean-Shift algorithm. But as a main and novel theoretical result compared to Mean-Shift, this paper shows that the sole fixed-points of the identified function tend to be the cluster centroids if both the number of measurements per cluster and the distances between centroids are large enough. As a second contribution, this paper introduces the Wald kernel for clustering. This kernel is defined as the p-value of the Wald hypothesis test for testing the mean of a Gaussian. As such, the Wald kernel measures the plausibility that a measurement vector belongs to a given cluster and it scales better with the dimension of the measurement vectors than the usual Gaussian kernel. Finally, the proposed theoretical framework allows us to derive a new clustering algorithm called CENTREx that works by estimating the fixed-points of the identified function. As Mean-Shift, CENTREx requires no prior knowledge of the number of clusters. It relies on a Wald hypothesis test to significantly reduce the number of fixed points to calculate compared to the Mean-Shift algorithm, thus resulting in a clear gain in complexity. Simulation results on synthetic and real data sets show that CENTREx has comparable or better performance than standard clustering algorithms K-means and Mean-Shift, even when the covariance matrices are not perfectly known.

1
Introduction

Clustering is an unsupervised learning task that consists in dividing a data set into clusters such that data inside a cluster are similar with each other, and different from data belonging to other clusters
(
26
;
2
)
.

Clustering is commonly used in various Machine Learning tasks
(
14
;
47
;
32
)
, including document classification
(
49
)
, information retrieval, or data set segmentation
(
24
)
. It is also widely employed in multisensor signal processing applications, as highlighted by
1
.

Each clustering algorithm is based on underlying assumptions that determine the prior information required by the algorithm. For example, K-means
(
26
)
, spectral clustering
(
51
)
, and WARD clustering
(
54
)
algorithms require the number
K
of clusters
(
26
)
. Given that it can be challenging to determine practically the appropriate value of
K
, numerous techniques have been proposed to jointly estimate
K
and perform clustering: among others,
37
;
22
;
31
;
11
;
20
;
30
;
41
;
46
.
In contrast, algorithms like DB-SCAN
(
13
)
, OPTICS
(
3
)
, and BIRCH
(
60
)
, do not need any prior knowledge of
K
. Instead, they depend on parameters such as the maximum distance between two points in a cluster or the minimum number of points per cluster. These parameters actually characterize the intra-cluster density, and they have a significant influence on the clustering performance.

Mean-Shift
(
9
)
is another clustering method requiring no knowledge of
K
. It was shown to achieve good clustering performance in various applications
(
35
;
7
;
8
;
25
;
57
;
58
)
. The Mean-Shift algorithm is derived by formulating clustering as a kernel density estimation (KDE) problem over a data set, with a fixed kernel. Mean-Shift estimates one after the other the modes of the probability density function (pdf) of the data set, which are then considered as the cluster centroids. To do so, a location M-estimator
(
9
;
48
)
is derived from a cost function
J_{N}

(
9
, Sec. 2.6)
, where
N
is the number of data in the data set. The critical points (the points for which the derivative equals
0
) of
J_{N}
are shown to be the fixed-points of a certain function
\bm{g}_{N}
; these fixed-points are evaluated by a standard iterative calculation and considered as estimates of the sought centroids.

The Mean-Shift algorithm has three main drawbacks.

1.

A substantial effort has been made to investigate the convergence of the fixed-point iterative calculation process
(
17
;
18
;
16
;
56
)
. The convergence was established in the one-dimensional case only by
17
. For higher dimensions, a restrictive sufficient convergence condition was proposed for Gaussian kernels in
18
, while necessary and sufficient convergence conditions were provided for more complex variants of the Mean-Shift iterative procedure by
16
and
56
.
However, investigating this convergence is not sufficient in itself, given that the following two questions are also crucial for the theoretical analysis of Mean-Shift and its variants: (i) do the fixed-points of
\bm{g}_{N}
, which we know to be critical points, minimize the cost function
J_{N}
? (ii) do the minimizers of
J_{N}
approximate the centroids with respect to a certain criterion?
In this paper, we directly prove that the fixed-points of an extended and randomized version of
\bm{g}_{N}
converge to the centroids in a certain probabilistic sense introduced in this paper, without having to show that these fixed-points minimize
J_{N}
.

2.

The Mean-Shift algorithm depends on a kernel function
w
which weighs the contribution of each data point in the function
g_{N}
. Most works use a Gaussian kernel that depends on a parameter
h
called bandwidth
(
55
;
9
;
18
)
. The value of
h
significantly affects the clustering performance and various methods have been proposed to estimate it
(
10
;
43
)
.
However, although these approaches yield satisfactory clustering results for small data dimensions (
\mathrm{d}<20
), the Gaussian kernel does not scale well as the data dimension
\mathrm{d}
increases
(
23
)
. Thus, the secondary objective of this paper is to introduce an alternative kernel that can better accommodate higher dimensions. We propose to choose the kernel as the p-value of an hypothesis test to decide on whether a data point belongs to a certain cluster.

3.

The third issue is that the Mean-Shift algorithm estimates the fixed points of
\bm{g}_{N}
by merging the results of
N
iterative procedures, each initialized with a different data point among the
N
available. Alternatively, we propose to apply a hypothesis test after each centroid estimation to remove from the set of initializers all data points that likely belong to the current cluster.

2
Contributions and paper outline

While Mean-Shift initially considered Gaussian vectors all with identical diagonal covariance matrix, we address the case where each data vector may have its own specific covariance matrix (heteroscedasticity). By making such an hypothesis, we also have in mind the problem of clustering measurements collected through a network of sensors where thermal noise spectral properties can differ from one sensor to another. In addition, we analyze to what extent exact knowledge of the covariance matrices can be mitigated. Of course, we also discuss the simpler and more usual case where all covariance matrices are identical and diagonal
(
9
;
55
;
12
)
. Our main contributions can then be summarized as follows:

1.

We introduce a new cost function
J_{N}
encompassing the case where each data vector has its own covariance matrix. This cost function generalizes those considered by
9
and
55
. As with Mean-Shift, the critical points of our new cost function
J_{N}
are the fixed-points of a function
\bm{g}_{N}
obtained by differentiating
J_{N}
. For the general case of heteroscedastic Gaussian measurement vectors, we then show the strong theoretical result that each centroid is the fixed-point of a randomized version
\bm{G}_{N}
of
\bm{g}_{N}
, when the number
N
of data and the distances between this centroid and all the others tend to infinity. This result stated in Theorem
1
follows from a novel theoretical analysis that consists of studying the convergence of
\bm{G}_{N}
in a specific probabilistic sense defined in the paper. The approach is non-Bayesian in that, unlike Gaussian Mixture Models (GMM)
(
39
)
, the probabilities that the data belong to the different clusters are not required in the derivation. The parametric form of our cost function also induces that the result holds even when the data covariance matrices are unknown and approximated.

2.

We introduce a novel kernel
w
for clustering Gaussian clusters. This kernel is hereafter called the Wald kernel because it derives from the p-value of Wald’s Uniformly Best Constant Power (UBCP) test
(
52
, Proposition 3)
for testing the mean of a Gaussian. By construction, this kernel scales better with the data dimension than the standard Gaussian kernel.

3.

From the asymptotic result of Theorem
1
, the centroids of heteroscedastic Gaussian clusters can be estimated by the fixed-points of
\bm{G}_{N}
. Hence, we introduce a novel clustering algorithm called CENTREx that estimates the cluster centroids, similarly to Mean-Shift, by looking for the fixed-points of the function
\bm{g}_{N}
, seen as a realization of
\bm{G}_{N}
. CENTREx relies on Wald’s UBCP test
(
52
, Proposition 3)
again, to eliminate from the set of potential initializers all the measurement vectors that are close enough to an already estimated fixed-point. This elimination strategy greatly reduces the complexity of CENTREx compared to Mean-Shift. Note that, similarly to Mean-Shift, CENTREx can be used with the Gaussian kernel or the Wald kernel. In the special case of clusters with identical covariance matrices proportional to identity, we derive a Maximum Likelihood Estimator (MLE) of the variance of the data. This MLE can be used by CENTREx and the Mean-Shift algorithm to perform the clustering.

We provide numerical simulation results to assess CENTREx. The results show that CENTREx has the same clustering performance as Mean-Shift when the two algorithms use the same kernel. However, CENTREx provides a significant reduction in complexity. Furthermore, we observe that the Wald kernel outperforms the Gaussian kernel, particularly when dealing with high-dimensional data (
\mathrm{d}=100
). We further evaluate the robustness of CENTREx when using the Wald kernel. For identical covariance matrices proportional to identity, our results show that the proposed MLE of the variance of the data incurs only a minor performance degradation compared to the scenario where this variance is known. We also analyze the case of diagonal covariance matrices that are not scaled identity and identify effective strategies for selecting the variance parameters for the data vectors. All these experimental results pinpoint the relevance of Theorem
1
, even in non-asymptotic situations, and provide evidence of the robustness and computational efficiency of the proposed approach.

The outline of the paper is as follows. Section
3
gives our notation and formally describes the considered Gaussian model. Section
4
provides the cost function. Section
5
presents the theoretical analysis for centroid estimation. Section
6
introduces the Wald kernel. Section
7
describes the proposed algorithm CENTREx. Section
8
provides the numerical results.

3
Notation and cluster model

This section introduces pieces of notation used throughout the paper. It also provides the statistical model of clusters used to address the centroid estimation problem in Section
4
. This model will be extended in Section
5.1.1
to achieve our convergence analysis.

Sets, functions and sequences.

Throughout the paper,
\mathbb{N}
is the set of positive integers. Given two positive integers
p
and
q
such that
p\leqslant q
,
\llbracket p,q\rrbracket
denotes the set of integers
\{p,p+1,\ldots,q\}
and similarly,
\llbracket q,\infty\llbracket
( resp.
\rrbracket q,\infty\llbracket\,
) is the set of all integers
n\geqslant q
(resp.
n>q
).

\mathbb{R}
is the set of all real values. Given two elements
a
and
b
of the extended real line such that
-\infty\leqslant a\leqslant b\leqslant\infty
,
{]a,b[}
is the interval of all
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that
a<x<b
.

The cardinality of a set
\mathbb{X}
is denoted by
\textrm{card}(\mathbb{X})
. Given any two sets
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{Y}
, the notation
f:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{Y}
designates a function. Given such a function
f:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{Y}
, the inverse image
\{x\in\mathbb{X}:f(x)\in B\}
of
B\subset\mathbb{Y}
by
f
will be denoted as
f^{-1}(B)
, and the direct image
\{f(x):x\in A\}
of
A\subset\mathbb{X}
by
f
is denoted as
f(A)
.

A function defined on
\mathbb{N}
, or on
\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, is called a sequence. The limsup of a sequence of real values
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and the limsup of a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of functions valued in
\mathbb{R}
are denoted by
\underset{n\to\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\,x_{n}
and
\underset{n\to\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\,f_{n}
, respectively.

Vectors and matrices.

Throughout the paper, we consider some fixed
\mathrm{d}\in\mathbb{N}
to designate the common dimension of all the random vectors encountered below. The space of all
\mathrm{d}
-dimensional real column vectors is denoted by
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
. Bold characters are used to denote vectors, matrices and, more generally, functions valued in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
.

In this respect, given a set
\mathbb{X}
, a sequence of functions
\bm{f}_{n}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
is denoted by a bold character
\bm{f}=(\bm{f}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. We also deal with functions
\bm{f}
, still denoted by bold characters, that assign to each
x\in\mathbb{X}
a sequence
\bm{f}(x)
of functions valued in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
. In this case, for each
x\in\mathbb{X}
, the elements of the sequence
\bm{f}(x)
are denoted by
\bm{f}(x,n)
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, we have
\bm{f}(x)=(\bm{f}(x,n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where each function
\bm{f}(x,n)
is valued in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
.

We denote by
\operatorname{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\operatorname{GL}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathbb{R})
the set of all
\mathrm{d}\times\mathrm{d}
matrices with real coefficients and the General Linear Group of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, respectively. In the usual way,
\cdot^{\mathrm{T}}
denotes the transpose operator for vectors and matrices. The identity matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
is denoted by
\bm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}
. We write
\operatorname{diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\mathrm{d}})
for a
\mathrm{d}\times\mathrm{d}
diagonal matrix whose diagonal elements are the real numbers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\mathrm{d}}
. If the diagonal elements of
\bm{\Lambda}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda^{2}_{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2}_{\mathrm{d}})
are non-null, we set
\bm{\Lambda}^{-1/2}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\lambda_{\mathrm{d}}^{-1})
.

Given
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,\mathrm{d}\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket
,
(\bm{x})_{i}
designates the
i^{\text{th}}
coordinate or
\bm{x}
. Most often, we simply set
x_{i}=(\bm{x})_{i}
to alleviate the notation so that we generally write
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathrm{d}})^{\mathrm{T}}
. We also write
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\mathrm{d}}
to shorten the notation, which does not explicitly include the transpose operator. The reader must thus keep in mind that we only handle column vectors. According to these conventions, for any
\bm{x}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, any
\bm{A}\in\operatorname{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathbb{R})
and any
i\in\llbracket 1,\mathrm{d}\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket
,
(\bm{A}\bm{x})_{i}
designates the
i^{\text{th}}
coordinate of
\bm{A}\bm{x}
so that
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\left((\bm{A}\bm{x})_{1},\ldots,(\bm{A}\bm{x})_{\mathrm{d}}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}
. For vectors
\bm{A}\bm{x}
with coordinates given by rather long formulas, it will be more convenient to write
\bm{A}\bm{x}=\left((\bm{A}\bm{x})_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\mathrm{d}}
, again without recalling the transposition.

Mahalanobis norm.

Throughout, we will extensively use Mahalanobis norms. We recall that the Mahalanobis norm
\nu_{\bm{S}}
, associated with a given
\mathrm{d}\times\mathrm{d}
symmetric positive-definite matrix
\bm{S}
, is defined by

\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}},\nu_{\bm{S}}(\bm{x})=\sqrt{\bm{x}^{T}\bm{S}^{-1}\bm{x}}\,.

(1)

If
\bm{S}
is the identity matrix
\bm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}
, then the Mahalanobis norm
\nu_{\bm{S}}
is the Euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
. As usual, this Euclidean norm will be denoted by
\|\cdot\|
.

Given a
\mathrm{d}\times\mathrm{d}
symmetric positive-definite matrix
{\bm{S}}
, we recall the existence of an eigen decomposition

\bm{S}=\bm{U}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{U}^{\mathrm{T}}\,,

(2)

where
\bm{\Lambda}=\text{diag}(\lambda^{2}_{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2}_{\mathrm{d}})
is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal values are the eigenvalues
\lambda^{2}_{1},\ldots,\lambda^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}
of
\bm{S}
, and the columns of
\bm{U}
are the corresponding eigenvectors.

Random vectors.

Random vectors are denoted with capital letters. Throughout, all random vectors are assumed to be defined with respect to the same probability space denoted by
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
. By
\mathrm{d}
-dimensional real random vector, we mean any function
\bm{Z}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
measurable with respect to
\mathcal{A}
and the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
. A random variable is a random vector valued in
\mathbb{R}
. The set of all
\mathrm{d}
-dimensional real random vectors is denoted
\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}})
, without recalling the underlying
\sigma
-algebras to simplify the notation. We simply write
\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
when
\mathrm{d}=1
. According to our conventions above for vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, given
\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}})
, we write
Z_{i}=(\bm{Z})_{i}
and thus
\bm{Z}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\mathrm{d}})^{\mathrm{T}}
. In the same vein, given
\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}})
,
\bm{A}\in\operatorname{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathbb{R})
and
i\in\llbracket 1,\mathrm{d}\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket
, we write
(\bm{A}\bm{Z})_{i}
for the
i^{\text{th}}
coordinate of
\bm{A}\bm{Z}
and thus
\bm{A}\bm{Z}=\left((\bm{A}\bm{Z})_{1},\ldots,(\bm{A}\bm{Z})_{\mathrm{d}}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}
, or even
\bm{A}\bm{Z}=\left((\bm{A}\bm{Z})_{i}\right)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\mathrm{d}}
in case of coordinates with heavy expressions. If
\bm{Z}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
is a random vector such that
\mathbb{P}\left[\,\bm{Z}\in B\,\right]=1
for some Borel set
B
of
\mathbb{R}
, we write
\bm{Z}\in B
(
\mathbb{P}
-a.s) for
almost surely with respect to
\mathbb{P}
. For any random variable
X\in\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
, the essential supremum of
|X|
will hereafter be denoted by
|X|_{\infty}
. As often in the literature, we write cdf for cumulative distribution function. As already mentioned in the introduction, we write pdf for probability density function.

3.1
Cluster model

Throughout the paper, we consider a Gaussian model for the data
(
6
;
55
;
15
;
22
;
9
)
.

At this stage, we assume that we are given
N\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence
(\bm{Y}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket}
of
\mathrm{d}
-dimensional real Gaussian random vectors defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
. In what follows, these random vectors are called measurement vectors and we suppose that the data to cluster are their realizations
\bm{y}_{1}=\bm{Y}_{1}(\omega)
, …,
\bm{y}_{N}=\bm{Y}_{N}(\omega)
for some
\omega\in\Omega
. Given
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
\bm{C}_{n}
denotes the covariance matrix of
\bm{Y}_{n}
. We then define a sequence of centroids as follows.

Definition 1
.

Given
K\in\mathbb{N}
, a sequence of centroids is any injective map
\bm{\Phi}:\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, the centroids being the
K
vectors
\bm{\Phi}(1),\ldots,\bm{\Phi}(K)
. The set of all sequences of centroids is denoted by
{\mathbb{S}}
.

Next, we assume the existence of a surjective finite sequence
\kappa=(\kappa_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket}
valued in
\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
such that

\forall n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket,\bm{Y}_{n}\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\Phi}(\kappa_{n}),\bm{C}_{n})\,.

(3)

The surjectivity of the sequence
\kappa
is required to guarantee the existence of at least one random vector
\bm{Y}_{n}
whose expectation is
\bm{\Phi}(k)
. With this definition, the
k^{\text{th}}
cluster is the set of all measurement vectors
\bm{Y}_{n}
whose expectation equals
\bm{\Phi}(k)
, and no cluster is empty.

By clustering, we mean that we aim to estimate the centroids issued from
\bm{\Phi}
. We assume no prior knowledge of the number
K
of clusters.

4
Centroid estimation

In this section, we first introduce a new cost function to estimate
\bm{\Phi}
from the measurement vectors
(\bm{Y}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
. We show that the centroids
\bm{\Phi}(k)
can be estimated by calculating the fixed-points of a certain function
\bm{g}_{N}
obtained after differentiating this cost function. We will see that the
mean-shift

(
9
)
is a particular case of
\bm{g}_{N}
.

4.1
Cost function for centroid estimation

Given an injective map
\bm{\Theta}:\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
and a realization
(\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N})
of
(\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{N})
, we introduce the following cost function
J
:

J(\bm{\Theta})=\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{R}(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k)))\,.

(4)

In this equation,
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is an increasing and differentiable function that verifies
\mathcal{R}(x)=0\Rightarrow x=0
, and
({{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket}
is a sequence of symmetric positive-definite matrices. Although the cost function
J
involves the number
K
of clusters, we will see in Section
4.2
that the derivation of the critical points of
J
does not depend on
K
.

Remark 1
.

If the covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
are known, a natural choice consists of setting
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=\bm{C}_{n}
for each
n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. Otherwise, our cost function allows us to use estimates
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
for these covariance matrices.

Comparatively, two cost functions are introduced for Mean-Shift
(
9
)
. The first one is given by a
multivariate kernel density estimator
to be maximized with respect to the vectors
\bm{\Theta}(k)
, following a KDE approach
(
53
)
. The second one is expressed as a
location estimator
to be minimized with respect to the vectors
\bm{\Theta}(k)
, following an M-estimation approach
(
62
)
. Interestingly, it is shown in
9
that both cost functions lead to the same theoretical framework and clustering algorithm. The cost function of
Equation 4
generalizes those introduced in
53
,
9
,
55
, to the case of possibly different covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
for each measurement vector
\bm{Y}_{n}
. In the next section, we proceed as in previous works by differentiating the cost function
J
above.

4.2
Critical points of the cost function

In order to estimate the centroids, we would like to minimize the cost function of
Equation 4
among all injective maps
\bm{\Theta}:\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
. For this, we follow the same steps as
9
and first calculate the gradient of
J
. Since the risk function
\mathcal{R}
is differentiable, we have, for any
k\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,

\partial_{\bm{\Theta}(k)}J(\bm{\Theta})=\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}\partial_{\bm{\Theta}(k)}\Big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\Big)\times\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\left(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\right)\,,

where
\partial_{\bm{\Theta}(k)}J(\bm{\Theta})
is the partial derivative of
J(\bm{\Theta})
with respect to
\bm{\Theta}(k)
. Since

\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))=(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))^{\mathrm{T}}{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\,,

it follows from standard matrix differentiation
(
38
, Sec. 2.4)
that, for any
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,

\partial_{\bm{\Theta}(k)}\Big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\Big)=-2{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\,,

so that

\partial_{\bm{\Theta}(k)}J(\bm{\Theta})=-2\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\left(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\right){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\,,

(5)

where the derivative

w=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}

(6)

of
\mathcal{R}
is hereafter called the kernel. We henceforth assume that
w
is valued in
{]0,\infty[}
. As a consequence, because each
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}
is itself symmetric and positive-definite,

\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\big){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}\in\operatorname{GL}_{\mathrm{d}}(\mathbb{R})\,.

It thus follows from
Equation 5
that, for all
k\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
\partial_{\bm{\Theta}(k)}J(\bm{\Theta})=0
if and only if

\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\big){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}\right)^{-1}\times\,\,\,\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\Theta}(k))\big){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}\bm{y}_{n}=\bm{\Theta}(k)\,.

(7)

Hence, finding an injective map
\bm{\Theta}:\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
satisfying
Equation 7
amounts to looking for the fixed-points of the function
\bm{g}_{N}
defined for every
\bm{\varphi}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
by setting

\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi})=\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\varphi})\big){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}\right)^{-1}\times\,\,\,\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\varphi})\big){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}\bm{y}_{n}\,.

(8)

The function
\bm{g}_{N}
will hereafter be called the
mean-shift function
for the following reason. Consider the particular case where there exists a positive real number
h
such that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\bm{C}_{n}={{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=h^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
. In this case, for every
\bm{\varphi}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, we have:

\hskip-8.5359pt\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi})=\dfrac{\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\left({\|\,\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\varphi}\,\|^{2}}/{h^{2}}\right)\bm{y}_{n}}{\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\left({\|\,\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\varphi}\,\|^{2}}/{h^{2}}\right)}\,.

(9)

The
mean-shift
identified by
9
after differentiating the cost function is thus a particular case of
\bm{g}_{N}
.
However, no theoretical evidence was given by
9
to justify that the fixed points of this function are the cluster centroids
\bm{\Phi}(k)
.

We could envisage using methods from conventional optimization theory to study whether these fixed points are minimizers of the cost function
J
, for instance by verifying conditions on the Hessian of
J
. However, such methods are difficult to apply here, due to the probabilistic model for the vectors
\bm{Y}_{n}
. We thus develop below an alternative approach that involves studying the convergence in a specific probabilistic sense of a randomized version
\bm{G}_{N}
of
\bm{g}_{N}
.
As a novel and strong theoretical result, we show that, at least asymptotically, the centroids
\bm{\Phi}(k)
are the sole fixed points of this random function.

5
Convergence analysis

This section presents our main theoretical result stated in Theorem
1
. This result is asymptotic and involves countably infinite sequences of measurement vectors. To ease the reading of this theorem, we first introduce and comment most of the material required to state it.

5.1
Preliminary material

Here, we provide all the material required to define in Section
5.2
the convergence criterion used in Theorem
1
. We begin with the modeling of a sequence of measurement vectors as a function of a sequence of centroids. We also define our randomized mean-shift function, of which
\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi})
of
Equation 8
is a realization. We then introduce the notion of distance between a sequence of centroids and a fixed vector. Finally, we recall some definitions and properties of bases and limits to define our convergence criterion in Section
5.2
.

5.1.1
Centroids, clusters and measurement vector models

Since our analysis requires making
N
tend to infinity, we need a countably infinite — in short, denumerable — sequence of measurement vectors. We denote it by
(\bm{Y}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

We consider a sequence of centroids in the form of an injective map
\bm{\Phi}
, as introduced in Definition
1
. To extend the initial model of
Equation 3
to the case of a sequence of measurement vectors
(\bm{Y}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we must move from the surjective finite sequence
\kappa
of Section
3.1
to a surjective denumerable sequence
\kappa=(\kappa_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of integers valued in
\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
. Given that the convergence criterion considered in Theorem
1
requires
\bm{\Phi}
to be considered as a variable, the following definitions allow to associate unequivocally centroids to measurement vectors. We remind that
{\mathbb{S}}
is the set of all sequences of centroids as defined in Definition
1
.

Definition 2
.

Given a sequence
\bm{C}=(\bm{C}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of symmetric positive-definite matrices and a surjective sequence
\kappa=(\kappa_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
valued in
\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}}
is defined as the set of sequences
\bm{W}=(\bm{W}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
{\mathrm{d}}
-dimensional real Gaussian random vectors for which there exists
\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}
such that

\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\bm{W}_{n}\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\Phi}({\kappa_{n}}),\bm{C}_{n})\,.

(10)

By definition, given
\bm{W}\in{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}}
, there is a unique
\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}
satisfying
Equation 10
. Indeed, if
\bm{\Phi}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{S}}
satisfies
Equation 10
,
\bm{\Phi}^{\prime}(\kappa_{n})=\bm{\Phi}(\kappa_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\kappa
is surjective, given any
k\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, there exists at least one
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k=\kappa_{n}
, which implies that
\bm{\Phi}^{\prime}(k)=\bm{\Phi}(k)
. Whence the next definition.

Definition 3
.

Given a sequence
\bm{C}=(\bm{C}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of symmetric positive-definite matrices and a surjective sequence
\kappa=(\kappa_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
valued in
\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, we define

\operatorname{CtrSeq}_{\kappa,\bm{C}}:{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}}\to{\mathbb{S}}

as the function assigning to each given
\bm{W}=(\bm{W}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}}
the unique sequence of centroids
\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}
satisfying
Equation 10
.

We denote by
{\operatorname{Models}({\mathbb{S}},{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}})}
the set of all functions
\bm{Y}:{\mathbb{S}}\to{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}}
such that:

{\forall\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}},\operatorname{CtrSeq}_{\kappa,\bm{C}}(\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi}))=\bm{\Phi}}\,.

(11)

With the definitions introduced above, we enrich our model and notation for the measurement vectors to explicitly show the variables with respect to which the asymptotic result will be established. Specifically, consider any
\bm{Y}\in{\operatorname{Models}({\mathbb{S}},{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}})}
and any
\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}
. The sequence
\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi})=({\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},n}\right)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is assigned by
\bm{Y}
to
\bm{\Phi}
. According to
Equation 11
and
Equation 10
, we thus have:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N},{\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},n}\right)}\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\Phi}({\kappa_{n}}),\bm{C}_{n})\,.

(12)

From now on, the measurement vectors are modelled and denoted by the random vectors
{\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},n}\right)}
, and
\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi})
is the sequence of measurement vectors, on the basis of which we will estimate the centroids.

5.1.2
Randomized mean-shift function

Let
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}=({{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be any sequence of symmetric positive-definite matrices. For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the first
N
elements of
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}
can be used to specify
\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi})
in
Equation 8
. Given any sequence
\bm{W}\!=\!(\bm{W}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of elements of
\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}})
and any
\bm{\varphi}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, set

\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}(\bm{\varphi},\bm{W})=\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{W}_{n}-\bm{\varphi})\big){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}\right)^{-1}\times\,\,\,\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{W}_{n}-\bm{\varphi})\big){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}\bm{W}_{n}\,.

(13)

Given any
\bm{Y}\in{\operatorname{Models}({\mathbb{S}},{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}})}
, the definition of
\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi})
given by
Equation 8
can thus be extended to the random function

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hskip-11.38092pt\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}(\bm{\varphi},\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi}))=\left(\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}({\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},n}\right)}-\bm{\varphi})\big){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}\right)^{-1}\\
\hskip 142.26378pt\times\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}({\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},n}\right)}-\bm{\varphi})\big){{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}^{\,-1}{\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},n}\right)}\,.\end{array}

(14)

The random function
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}(\bm{\varphi},\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi}))
is the randomized mean-shift function we need in what follows. In particular,
\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi})
is the realization of
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}(\bm{\varphi},\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi}))
when
\bm{y}_{1}={\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},1}\right)}(\omega),\bm{y}_{2}={\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},2}\right)}(\omega),\ldots,\bm{y}_{N}={\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},N}\right)}(\omega)
for some
\omega\in\Omega
. Unlike
\bm{g}_{N}
, the notation
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}
mentions explicitly the sequence
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}
appearing in its expression. This sequence can be regarded as a parameter of
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}
. This heavier notation is needed because the statement of Theorem
1
involves both
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}
and another sequence
{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}
of covariance matrices to parameterize the randomized mean-shift function.

5.1.3
Distance between a sequence of centroids and a fixed vector

In Theorem
1
, we make the distance between a given centroid and all the other ones grow to infinity. We need Definitions
4
and
5
below to arrive at Definition
6
used to define the distance between a centroid and all others.

Definition 4
.

Given
k_{0}\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
and
\bm{\varphi}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
,
{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}\subset{\mathbb{S}}
is the set of all
\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}
whose
k_{0}^{\text{th}}
element is
\bm{\varphi}_{0}
:

{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}=\left\{\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}:\bm{\Phi}(k_{0})=\bm{\varphi}_{0}\right\}\,.

We thus use
{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}
to fix the
k_{0}^{\text{\scriptsize{th}}}
centroid at
\bm{\varphi}_{0}
and make all the other ones move away from it. Proving that the sole fixed point of
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}(\bm{\varphi},\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi}))
is asymptotically
\bm{\varphi}_{0}
, when the distance between this centroid and all the other ones tend to infinity, requires considering the vector measurement models
\bm{Y}\in{\operatorname{Models}({\mathbb{S}},{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}})}
whose domain is
{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}
. Thence the following definition and notation.

Definition 5
.

Given any
k_{0}\!\in\!\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
and any
\bm{\varphi}_{0}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
,
{\operatorname{Models}({\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}},{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}})}
is defined as the set of all functions
\bm{Y}:{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}\to{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}}
such that

{\forall\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}},\operatorname{CtrSeq}_{\kappa,\bm{C}}\left(\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi})\right)=\bm{\Phi}}\,.

(15)

To make centroids other than the
k_{0}^{\text{\scriptsize{th}}}
move away from a given
\bm{\varphi}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, we use the following definition.

Definition 6
.

Given
k_{0}\!\in\!\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
and
\bm{\varphi}_{0}\!\in\!\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, we define the function
D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}:{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}\to{{[0,\infty[}}
for any
\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}
by setting

D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})=\min\big\{\|\bm{\Phi}(k)-\bm{\varphi}_{0}\|:k\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket\!\setminus\!\{k_{0}\}\big\}\,.

(16)

Given
\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}
,
D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})
measures the distance between the centroids
\bm{\Phi}(k)
with
k\neq k_{0}
and
\bm{\varphi}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
. We make this distance increase to infinity by using an appropriate base in
{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}
, as stated in the next paragraph.

5.1.4
Bases and limits

We recall that a base
\mathscr{B}
— or filter base — in a given set
\mathbb{X}
is a nonempty subset of the power set of
\mathbb{X}
such that
(
61
, p. 128, Definition 11)
:

(i)

\emptyset\notin\mathscr{B}
;

(ii)

For all
(B_{1},B_{2})\in\mathscr{B}\times\mathscr{B}
, there exists
B\in\mathscr{B}
such that
B\subset B_{1}\cap B_{2}
.

Consider a base
\mathscr{B}
in a set
\mathbb{X}
. Since all norms on
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
are equivalent, it follows from
61
that
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
is the limit of a function
\bm{f}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
over
\mathscr{B}
, which we write
\lim_{\mathscr{B}}\bm{f}=\zeta
, if

\hskip-5.69046pt\forall\eta\in{]0,\infty[},\exists B\in\mathscr{B},\forall x\in\mathbb{X},\left(x\in B\Rightarrow\|\bm{f}(x)-\zeta\|<\eta\right)\,.

(17)

As a basic and illustrative example,
\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{N}}=\{\,\rrbracket n,\infty\llbracket:n\in\mathbb{N}\,\}
is, in
\mathbb{N}
, the base with respect to which we define the convergence of a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of real values when
n
tends to
\infty
.

The following facts will be helpful. If
\mathscr{B}
is a base in a set
\mathbb{X}
and
\gamma:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
is a bijection, then the set
\gamma(\mathscr{B})=\{\gamma(B):B\in\mathscr{B}\}
of all the direct images of the elements of
\mathscr{B}
is a base in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
and
Equation 17
implies that, for any
\bm{f}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
with a limit over
\mathscr{B}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathscr{B}}\bm{f}=\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma(\mathscr{B})}(\bm{f}\circ\gamma^{-1})\,.

(18)

Considering any set
\mathbb{X}
, if
H:\mathbb{X}\to{[0,\infty[}
is a function such that
H^{-1}\big(\,{]r,\infty[}\,\big)\neq\emptyset
for all
r\in{[0,\infty[}
, then

\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{X}}(H)=\Big\{H^{-1}\big(\,{]r,\infty[}\,\big):r\in{[0,\infty[})\Big\}

(19)

is a base in
\mathbb{X}
. Given a function
\bm{f}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, we write
\displaystyle\lim_{H(x)\to\infty}\bm{f}(x)=\zeta
to mean that
\lim_{\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{X}}(H)}\bm{f}=\zeta
. Therefore, according to
Equation 17
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{{H}(x)\to\infty}\bm{f}(x)=\zeta\Leftrightarrow\Big(\,\forall\eta\in{]0,\infty[},\exists\,r\in{[0,\infty[},\forall x\in\mathbb{X},{H}(x)>r\Rightarrow\|\bm{f}(x)-\zeta\|<\eta\,\Big)\,.

(20)

In addition, suppose that we have a bijection
\gamma:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
between two arbitrary sets and a function
H:\mathbb{X}\to{[0,\infty[}
such that, for any
r\in{[0,\infty[}
,
H^{-1}\big(\,{]r,\infty[}\,\big)\neq\emptyset
. We derive from the foregoing that
\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}(H\circ\gamma^{-1})
, defined according to
Equation 19
, equals
\gamma(\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{X}}(H))
and is a base in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
. It thus follows by using
Equation 18
and the writing conventions introduced above that, for any
f:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
for which
\displaystyle\lim_{H(x)\to\infty}\bm{f}(x)
exists, we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{H(x)\to\infty}\bm{f}(x)=\displaystyle\lim_{(H\circ\gamma^{-1})(y)\to\infty}(\bm{f}\circ\gamma^{-1})(y)\,.

(21)

Now, fix
k_{0}\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
and
\bm{\varphi}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
. If
{H}:{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}\to{[0,\infty[}
is a function such that
{H}^{-1}\big(\,{]r,\infty[}\,\big)\neq\emptyset
for any
r\in{[0,\infty[}
then

\mathscr{B}_{{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}}\!\left({{H}}\right)=\Big\{{H}^{-1}\big(\,{]r,\infty[}\,\big):r\in{[0,\infty[}\Big\}

(22)

is a base in
{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}
.
In particular, we identify

\hskip-9.95863pt\mathscr{B}_{{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}}\!\left({D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}}\right)=\Big\{\left(D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}\right)^{-1}\big(\,{]r,\infty[}\,\big):r\in{[0,\infty[}\Big\}

(23)

as the suitable base in
{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}
to study the limit of a function
\bm{f}:{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
when
D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})
tends to
\infty
. If
\bm{f}
has a limit
\zeta
over
\mathscr{B}_{{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}}\!\left({D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}}\right)
, we thus write

\displaystyle\lim_{D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})\to\infty}\bm{f}(\bm{\Phi})=\zeta

and it follows from
Equation 20
&
Equation 23
that

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hskip-8.5359pt\displaystyle\lim_{D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})\to\infty}\bm{f}(\bm{\Phi})=\zeta\\
\quad\,\Leftrightarrow\Big(\,\forall\eta\in{]0,\infty[},\exists\,r\in{[0,\infty[},\forall\,\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}},D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})>r\Rightarrow\|\bm{f}(\bm{\Phi})-\zeta\|<\eta\,\Big)\,.\end{array}

(24)

In Theorem
1
, we make
N
and
D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})
grow to
\infty
. This requires the specific convergence criterion introduced in the next section.

5.2
Essential convergence in limsup (ess.lim.sup)

The definition of
ess.lim.sup
is novel. It formalizes and extends the convergence criterion
(
34
, Sec. 2.1)
used to state
34
.

Define a parametric discrete random process as any function
\bm{F}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}})
, where
\mathbb{X}
is some set, typically a set of parameters. Such a function
\bm{F}
assigns to each pair
({x},N)\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{N}
a unique
\mathrm{d}
-dimensional real random vector
\bm{F}(x,N)\in\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}})
. For a given
\bm{Y}\!\in\!{\operatorname{Models}({\mathbb{S}},{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}})}
and a given
\bm{\varphi}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
, the function
(\bm{\Phi},N)\mapsto\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}(\bm{\varphi},\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi}))
defined in
Equation 14
is a parametric discrete random function defined on
{\operatorname{Models}({\mathbb{S}},{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}})}\times\mathbb{N}
.

Given any parametric discrete random process
\bm{F}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}})
, we now define the
ess.lim.sup
convergence of
\bm{F}(x,N)
over a base
\mathscr{B}
in
\mathbb{X}
when
N
tends to
\infty
as follows.

Definition 7
.

Let
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
and
\bm{F}:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}})
be a parametric discrete random process.
For any pair
(x,N)\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{N}
, set

\begin{array}[]{llll}\|\bm{F}(x,N)-\zeta\,\|:&\Omega&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
&\omega&\mapsto&\|\bm{F}(x,N)(\omega)-\zeta\,\|\,.\end{array}

Given a base
\mathscr{B}
in
\mathbb{X}
, we write

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{ess. lim. sup}}_{\mathscr{B}\,,\,N\to\infty}\bm{F}(x,N)=\zeta

and say that
\bm{F}(x,N)
converges essentially in limsup — or simply, in
ess.lim.sup
— to
\zeta
, over
\mathscr{B}
when
N
tends to
\infty
, if

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathscr{B}}\left|\,\underset{\,N\to\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\|\,\bm{F}(x,N)-\zeta\,\|_{\,}\right|_{\infty}=0\,.

With the same notation as in the definition above, we straightforwardly have

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{ess. lim. sup}}_{\mathscr{B}\,,\,N\to\infty}\bm{F}(x,N)=\zeta\,\Leftrightarrow\,\displaystyle\mathop{\text{ess. lim. sup}}_{\mathscr{B}\,,\,N\to\infty}\left(\bm{F}(x,N)-\zeta\right)=0\,.

(25)

Below, we consider the particular case where
\mathbb{X}={\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}
and
\mathscr{B}_{{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}}\!\left({D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}}\right)
defined by
Equation 23
are used to make
D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})
tend to
\infty
. Thus, with the notation introduced above, given
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
and any parametric discrete random process
\bm{F}:{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{M}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}})
, we have :

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{ess. lim. sup}}_{D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})\to\infty\,,\,N\to\infty}\bm{F}(\bm{\Phi},N)=\zeta\,\,\,\Leftrightarrow\displaystyle\lim_{D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})\to\infty}\left|\,\underset{\,N\to\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\|\,\bm{F}(\bm{\Phi},N)-\zeta\,\|_{\,}\right|_{\infty}=0\,.

(26)

5.3
Main result

In this section, we state our main theoretical result, namely Theorem
1
. This asymptotic result relies on the following assumptions, completing those made in Sections
3
and
4
. These assumptions are presented and commented below before stating the theorem.

Regarding the sequence
\kappa=(\kappa_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that assigns the
\kappa_{n}^{\text{th}}
centroid to the measurement vector number
n
, we make the following assumption.

Assumption 1
.

\kappa=(\kappa_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a surjective sequence of integers in
\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
for which there exists
a_{0}\in{]0,1[}
such that

\forall(k,N)\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket\times{\llbracket K,\infty\llbracket},a_{0}\leqslant\textrm{card}\left\{n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket:\kappa_{n}=k\right\}/N\,.

Comment

The existence of the uniform lower bound
a_{0}>0
for the proportion of measurement vectors belonging to a given cluster implies that the cardinality of each cluster cannot be
0
and will increase to infinity as the number
N
of data grows. Thereby, no cluster becomes negligible in cardinality with respect to the others as
N
tends to infinity.

Assumption 2
.

The kernel
w:{[0,\infty[}\to{]0,\infty[}
is a continuous and non-increasing function verifying

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}{tw(t)}=0\,.

Comment

We consider kernels that are smooth enough to achieve the estimation of the centroids. This assumption is mild enough to encompass a large class of possible kernels, among which the Gaussian one.

The covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
of the measurement vectors are not necessarily equal, even when they correspond to data within the same cluster. We however introduce some coherence between these covariance matrices. In this respect, we make the following assumption.

Assumption 3
.

The covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
are diagonalizable in the same orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
and there exists an interval
[\sigma^{\,2}_{\text{min}},\sigma^{\,2}_{\text{max}}]\subset{]0,\infty[}
to which their eigenvalues all belong.

Comment

The coherence between the covariance matrices is in the sense that they all have the same eigenvectors. In contrast, the spectra of the covariance matrices are not necessarily identical. The bounds
\sigma^{\,2}_{\text{min}}
and
\sigma^{\,2}_{\text{max}}
are the sole parameters needed in the sequel to analyze the impact of the eigenvalues on the randomized mean-shift function
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}
.

We need a sequence
({{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to define the randomized mean-shift function
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}(\bm{\varphi},\bm{W})
defined in
Equation 13
. Considering, as suggested in Remark
1
, that
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
is an estimate of
\bm{C}_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we make the following similar assumptions for the matrices
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
as for the matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
.

Assumption 4
.

The covariance matrices
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
are diagonalizable in the same orthonormal basis as the covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and there exists an interval
[{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\,2}_{\!\!\text{min}},{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\,2}_{\!\!\text{max}}]\subset{]0,\infty[}
to which all their eigenvalues belong.

Comment

Considering, according to Remark
1
, that the matrices
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
are estimates of the matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
, we assume that the former share the same eigenvectors as the latter. On the other hand, the eigenvalues of the matrices
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
can be unknown, but they must have bounded possible variations. Note that the interval of variations of these eigenvalues can differ from
[\sigma^{\,2}_{\text{min}},\sigma^{\,2}_{\text{max}}]
. As for
3
,
{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\,2}_{\!\!\text{min}}
and
{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\,2}_{\!\!\text{max}}
are the sole parameters used below to study how the eigenvalues impact
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}
.

The following consequences of
3
and
4
will be useful to state Theorem
1
. First, in what follows, when we are given any two sequences
\bm{C}=(\bm{C})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}=({{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of symmetric positive-definite matrices satisfying
3
and
4
, we can write that

\displaystyle\bm{C}_{n}=\bm{R}\bm{\Delta}_{n}\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\,,

(27a)

\displaystyle{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=\bm{R}{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!n}\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\,,

(27b)

where:

•

\bm{R}
is a
\mathrm{d}\times\mathrm{d}
orthogonal matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors of all the matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
and
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

•

For any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\bm{\Delta}_{n}
and
{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!n}
are diagonal matrices whose diagonal elements are the eigenvalues of
\bm{C}_{n}
and
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
, respectively.

According to
Equation 1
, the Mahalanobis norms
\nu_{\bm{C}_{n}}
and
\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}
associated with
\bm{C}_{n}
and
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
, respectively, are thus given by:

\displaystyle\nu_{\bm{C}_{n}}(\bm{x})=\nu_{\bm{\Delta}_{n}}\!(\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}{\bm{x}})\,,

(28a)

\displaystyle\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}(\bm{x})=\nu_{{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!n}}\!(\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{x})\,.

(28b)

Second, consider
\bm{W}=(\bm{W}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}}
(see Definition
2
) and set
\bm{\Phi}=\operatorname{CtrSeq}_{\kappa,\bm{C}}(\bm{W})
(see Definition
3
). From
Equation 10
&
Equation 27a
, we thus have:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{W}_{n}\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\Phi}(\kappa_{n}),\bm{\Delta}_{n})\,.

(29)

Since
\bm{\Delta}\!=\!(\bm{\Delta}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of diagonal positive-definite matrices and
\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}
, it follows from
Equation 29
that we can define the function:

\hskip-5.69046pt\begin{array}[]{lllll}\overline{\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}}:&{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}}&\to&{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{\Delta}}\\
&\bm{W}&\mapsto&(\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{W}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\,.\end{array}

(30)

With the material introduced above, we can now state our main theoretical result.

Theorem 1
.

Consider a sequence
\kappa\!=\!(\kappa_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of integers valued in
\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, a function
w:{[0,\infty[}\to{]0,\infty[}
and two sequences
\bm{C}=(\bm{C})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}=({{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of symmetric positive-definite matrices. If
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
are verified, then:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\hskip-56.9055pt\forall\,k_{0}\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket,\forall\,(\bm{\varphi}_{0},\bm{\varphi})\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}},\forall\,\bm{Y}\in{\operatorname{Models}({\mathbb{S}},{\operatorname{Seq}}_{\,\kappa,\bm{C}})},\\
\begin{array}[]{lll}\bm{\varphi}=\bm{\varphi}_{0}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\textbf{A})}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&\displaystyle\mathop{\text{ess. lim. sup}}_{D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})\to\infty\,,\,N\to\infty}\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}(\bm{\varphi},\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi}))=\bm{\varphi}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\textbf{B})}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&\displaystyle\mathop{\text{ess. lim. sup}}_{D^{\bm{\varphi}_{0}}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})\to\infty\,,\,N\to\infty}\bm{G}_{N}^{{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}}(\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varphi},\overline{\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}}(\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi})))=\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varphi}\,.\end{array}\end{array}

(31)

where
{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}=({\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is the sequence of matrices defined by
Equation 27b
, and
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}
and
\bm{G}_{N}^{{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}}
are calculated according to
Equation 14
.

Proof.

Because of its length and technicality, the proof is postponed to Appendix
A
.
∎

Suppose that
\bm{\Phi}:\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
is a sequence of centroids and that
(\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N})
is a realization of
(\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{N})
under the hypotheses of Section
3.1
. We readily have
\bm{\Phi}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{k_{0}}^{\bm{\Phi}(k_{0})}
for each
k_{0}\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
. To estimate
\bm{\Phi}
in practice via our algorithm CENTREx described in Section
7
, we will implicitly assume that the centroids
\bm{\Phi}(1),\bm{\Phi}(2),\ldots,\bm{\Phi}(K)
are sufficiently far away from each other to apply Theorem
1
iteratively for each
k_{0}\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
. We will thus consider that, given any
k_{0}\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
N
and
D^{\bm{\Phi}(k_{0})}_{k_{0}}(\bm{\Phi})
are large enough for
\bm{\Phi}(k_{0})
to be a fixed point of the realization
{\bm{g}_{N}}
of
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}(\cdot,\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi}))
, where
\bm{Y}(\bm{\Phi})=({\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},n}\right)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
{\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},1}\right)}=\bm{Y}_{1},{\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},2}\right)}=\bm{Y}_{2},\ldots,{\bm{Y}\!\left({\bm{\Phi},N}\right)}=\bm{Y}_{N}
. In the next section, we explore how the fixed-points of
{\bm{g}_{N}}
can be estimated iteratively.

Remark 2
.

According to
4
, the matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
and
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
share the same eigenvectors concatenated in
\bm{R}
. This may appear as a restrictive assumption. However, many works have shown that the Fourier coefficients and the wavelet and wavelet packet coefficients of stationary random signals are asymptotically uncorrelated (see for instance,
36
;
21
;
28
;
29
;
4
;
5
;
59
, among others). In this respect,
\overline{\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}}
models such a transform chosen in advance for its ability to diagonalize sufficiently well the covariance matrices of the measurement vectors. This is why, in the statement of Theorem
1
, we have two equivalences:
(\textbf{A})
involves the initial measurement vectors, whereas
(\textbf{B})
is applied to the outcome of the transform
\overline{\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}}
. In the latter case, estimating the cluster centroids in practice will be achieved by considering the transformed data
\bm{z}_{1}=\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{z}_{N}=\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{y}_{N}
and calculating the fixed points
\bm{\xi}_{1},\bm{\xi}_{2},\ldots
of the function

\begin{array}[]{lll}\hskip-14.22636pt{\widetilde{\bm{g}_{N}}(\bm{\xi})}=\left(\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{\xi})\big){\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!n}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\hskip-2.84544pt\times\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}w\big(\nu_{{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!n}}^{2}(\bm{z}_{n}-\bm{\xi})\big){\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!n}^{-1}\bm{z}_{n}\,.\end{array}

(32)

obtained by replacing
\bm{C}_{1},\ldots,\bm{C}_{N}
in
\bm{g}_{N}
by
{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!1},\ldots,{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!N}
, and estimating the centroids by
\bm{R}\bm{\xi}_{1},\bm{R}\bm{\xi}_{2},\ldots
.

5.4
Iterative estimation of the fixed-points

Theorem
1
justifies that the centroids can be estimated by calculating the fixed points of
{\bm{g}_{N}}
defined in
Equation 8
. One method widely employed in practice to estimate the fixed-points of a function
f
consists of iteratively computing
\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}=f(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)})
until a certain stopping criterion is reached
(
62
)
. Such an iterative computation is used by
9
to calculate the fixed-points of the
mean-shift

(
9
, Eq. 17)
. However, the proof that the resulting sequence
(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
converges is not correct in
9
, as shown by
16
, and remains an open problem
(
17
;
18
;
16
;
56
)
. Although the next result does not prove the convergence, it provides us with a means to devise a stopping criterion for the iterative procedure
\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}=\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)})
. This stopping criterion applies to the
mean-shift
as a particular case.

Proposition 1
.

Suppose that the function
w:{[0,\infty[}\to{]0,\infty[}
is differentiable, bounded and non-increasing. Let
(\bm{y}_{1},\bm{y}_{2},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N})
be
N
elements of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}={\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)})}
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{\varphi}^{(1)}
is arbitrarily chosen in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
then:

\forall\varepsilon>0,\exists\ell_{0}(\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{N},\forall L\in\mathbb{N},\forall\ell\in\mathbb{N},\ell\geqslant\ell_{0}(\varepsilon)\Rightarrow\|\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+L)}-\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)}\|\leqslant L\varepsilon\,.

(33)

Proof.

See Appendix
C
.
∎

Especially, for
L=1
, the proposition states that
\|\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}-\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)}\|
can be made arbitrarily small for large enough
\ell
, which suggests considering
\|\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}-\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)}\|\leqslant\epsilon
as a stopping criterion for the iterative procedure.
Numerical simulations will confirm that using this iterative procedure together with the identified criterion leads to correct centroid estimation, as also empirically observed in other clustering algorithms like Mean-Shift
(
9
)
.

From a practical point of view, there are several other issues to address so as to devise an efficient method to estimate the
K
centroids of the set
\mathscr{C}
. For instance, we must
decide how to initialize the recursive computation
\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}={\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)})}
, and how to find a way to retrieve not only one, but
K
fixed points, while the value of
K
is
a priori
unknown. The clustering algorithm introduced in Section
7
addresses these issues to correctly build
K
clusters on the basis of Theorem
1
and thus, without prior knowledge of
K
.

In addition, the theoretical results of Theorem
1
and Proposition
1
rely on the properties of the weight function
w
. This is why, before describing the clustering algorithm, we introduce a new weight function satisfying
2
and the assumptions of Proposition
1
.

6
The Wald kernel

Theorem
1
holds for a large class of weight functions
w
. As a first option, the Gaussian weight function
w(t)=\exp(-t/2h)
, for
t\in{[0,\infty[}
, considered in
55
,
9
,
18
, verifies the properties required in Theorem
1
and Proposition
1
.
However, the bandwidth parameter
h
of this weight function must be chosen empirically and its optimal value varies with the dimension
\mathrm{d}

(
23
)
. A poor choice of
h
can dramatically impact the performance of the clustering algorithms proposed in
55
and
9
. In contrast, we introduce a novel kernel whose expression explicitly depends on the dimension
\mathrm{d}
. This kernel is called the Wald kernel because it derives from the Wald test
(
52
)
for testing the mean of a Gaussian. Because of the specificity of the material needed to introduce the Wald kernel, we begin by recalling basics about the Wald test and its p-value.

6.1
The Wald test for testing the mean of a Gaussian

Let
\bm{X}\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\xi},\bm{S})
be a
\mathrm{d}
-dimensional Gaussian random vector, where the
\mathrm{d}\times\mathrm{d}
matrix
\bm{S}
is symmetric positive-definite.
Consider the problem of testing whether
\bm{X}
is centered or not, that is, the problem of infering whether
\bm{\xi}=0
or not when we are given a realization of
\bm{X}
. Equivalently, we also say that we test the null hypothesis
\mathcal{H}_{0}
that
\bm{\xi}=0
against its alternative
\mathcal{H}_{1}
that
\bm{\xi}\neq 0
. We summarize this problem as:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\text{{Observation:}}\,\bm{X}\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\xi},\bm{S})\,,\\
\text{Hypotheses:}\,\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\!\!\mathcal{H}_{0}:\bm{\xi}=0\,,\\
\!\!\mathcal{H}_{1}:\bm{\xi}\neq 0\,.\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

(34)

This corresponds to a binary hypothesis testing problem
(
27
)
. To solve it, consider all the (measurable) functions
\mathfrak{T}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}\to\{0,1\}
. Any such function is called a test. We say that test
\mathfrak{T}
accepts
\mathcal{H}_{0}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{1}
) if
\mathfrak{T}(\bm{X})=0
(resp.
\mathfrak{T}(\bm{X})=1
). The size of a test
\mathfrak{T}
for testing the mean of
\bm{X}
is
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{T}(\bm{X})=1]
when
\bm{\xi}=0
. The size of
\mathfrak{T}
is thus the probability of rejecting
\mathcal{H}_{0}
when this hypothesis holds true. The power of
\mathfrak{T}
for testing the mean of
\bm{X}
is
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{T}(\bm{X})=1]
when
\bm{\xi}\neq 0
. Thus, it is the probability of accepting
\mathcal{H}_{1}
when this hypothesis is true.

Among all possible tests, we are interested by those whose size does not exceed some value
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
for testing the mean of
\bm{X}
. Such tests are said to have level
\alpha
. Given
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
, set

\mu(\alpha)=\sqrt{\mathbb{F}^{-1}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}(1-\alpha)}\,,

(35)

where
\mathbb{F}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}
is the cumulative distribution function of the chi-squared distribution with
\mathrm{d}
degrees of freedom. According to
52
, the test defined for any
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
by

\mathfrak{T}_{\bm{S}}^{\alpha}(\bm{x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}0&\hbox{ if }&\nu_{\bm{S}}(\bm{x})\leqslant\mu(\alpha)\,,\\
1&\hbox{ if }&\nu_{\bm{S}}(\bm{x})>\mu(\alpha)\,.\end{array}\right.

(36)

has size
\alpha
for the problem described by
Equation 34
. This test is called the Wald test with size
\alpha
for testing the mean of
\bm{X}
. The Wald test
\mathfrak{T}_{\bm{S}}^{\alpha}
turns out to be optimal with respect to several optimality criteria and within several subsets of tests with level
\alpha

(
52
, Definition III & Proposition III, p. 450)
,
(
33
, Proposition 2)
. A full description of the optimal properties satisfied by the Wald test is unnecessary to introduce the Wald kernel. In contrast, the p-value of the Wald tests is key to define our weight function.

6.2
p-value of the Wald test

Let
\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}_{\bm{S}}
be the function that assigns the Wald test
\mathfrak{T}_{\bm{S}}^{\alpha}
to a given
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
. As detailed in Appendix
D
, we can define the p-value
(
27
, p. 63, Sec. 3.3)

\text{pval}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}_{\bm{S}}}(\bm{X})=\inf\big\{\alpha\in{]0,1[}:\mathfrak{T}_{\bm{S}}^{\alpha}(\bm{X})=1\big\}

and prove that

\text{pval}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}_{\bm{S}}}(\bm{X})=1-\mathbb{F}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}(\nu^{2}_{\bm{S}}(\bm{X}))

(37)

for
\bm{X}\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\xi},\bm{S})
.The function
\text{pval}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}_{\bm{S}}}(\bm{x})=1-\mathbb{F}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}(\nu^{2}_{\bm{S}}(\bm{x}))
decreases with
\nu_{\bm{S}}(\bm{x})
and tends to
0
(resp.
1
) when
\nu_{\bm{S}}(\bm{x})
increases (resp. decreases). Therefore, as mentioned in
27
,
\text{pval}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}_{\bm{S}}}(\bm{X})
can be seen as a measure of the plausibility of the null hypothesis
\mathcal{H}_{0}
when we observe
\bm{X}
: when
\nu_{\bm{S}}(\bm{X})
is large (resp. small),
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is likely to be false (resp. true) and accordingly,
\text{pval}_{\bm{S}}(\bm{X})
is close to
0
(resp.
1
).

6.3
Definition and properties of the Wald kernel

Consider a finite sequence
(\bm{Y}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket}
of measurements and a sequence
\bm{\Phi}
of centroids that satisfy the Gaussian cluster model of Section
3.1
. If
\bm{\Phi}
were known, we could test, for each pair
(k,n)\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, whether
\mathbb{E}\big[\bm{Y}_{n}\big]
is
\bm{\Phi}(k)
or not. This would amount to testing whether
\bm{Y}_{n}-\bm{\Phi}(k)
is centered or not. According to Section
6.2
, given
\alpha\in{]0,1[}
, we must use the Wald test
\mathfrak{T}^{\alpha}_{\bm{C}_{n}}
to perform this testing. The p-value
\text{pval}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}_{\bm{C}_{n}}}(\bm{Y}_{n}-\bm{\Phi}(k))
thus measures the plausibility that
\mathbb{E}\big[\bm{Y}_{n}\big]=\bm{\Phi}(k)
when we observe
\bm{Y}_{n}
, and we have:

\text{pval}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}_{\bm{C}_{n}}}(\bm{Y}_{n}-\bm{\Phi}(k))=1-\mathbb{F}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}\left(\nu_{\bm{C}_{n}}^{2}(\bm{Y}_{n}-\bm{\Phi}(k)\right)\,.

(38)

Suppose that the matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
are known. According to Remark
1
, we naturally set
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=\bm{C}_{n}
for each
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
in
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}
defined by
Equation 14
. In this case, the weighting coefficients in
\bm{G}_{N}^{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}}
are the terms
w\left(\nu_{\bm{C}_{n}}^{2}(\bm{Y}_{n}-\bm{\varphi})\right)
for
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
. In particular, when
\bm{\varphi}=\bm{\Phi}(k)
, it is desirable that the closer the weighting coefficient
w\left(\nu_{\bm{C}_{n}}^{2}(\bm{Y}_{n}-\bm{\Phi}(k))\right)
is to
1
(resp.
0
), the more likely
\mathbb{E}\big[\bm{Y}_{n}\big]=\bm{\Phi}(k)
is to be true (resp. false). Therefore, we choose
w
such that

w\left(\nu_{\bm{C}_{n}}^{2}(\bm{Y}_{n}-\bm{\Phi}(k)\right)=\text{pval}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}_{\bm{C}_{n}}}(\bm{Y}_{n}-\bm{\Phi}(k))\,.

By identification with
Equation 38
, we thus define the wald Kernel
w:{[0,\infty[}\to{]0,\infty[}
for any real value
t\geqslant 0
by setting

w(t)=1-\mathbb{F}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}(t)\,.

(39)

The next lemma shows that the Wald kernel satisfies the required assumptions of Theorem
1
and Proposition
1
.

Lemma 1
.

The Wald kernel defined by
Equation 39
is differentiable, positive, non-increasing, and verifies
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}tw(t)=0
.

Proof.

The properties of
\mathbb{F}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}
imply directly that the Wald kernel is positive, differentiable and decreasing. Now, it follows from the first equality in
50
that
\forall t\in{[0,\infty[}
,
w(t)=Q_{{\mathrm{d}}/2}(0,\sqrt{t})
where, for any pair
(a,b)
of non-negative real values,
Q_{{\mathrm{d}}/2}(a,b)
is the
generalized Marcum
Q
-function of order
\mathrm{d}/2
.
According to the second equality in
50
, we have

\forall t\in{[0,\infty[},w(t)=\frac{1}{2^{\mathrm{d}/2}\Gamma(\mathrm{d}/2)}\int_{t}^{\infty}x^{\mathrm{d}/2-1}e^{-x/2}dx\,,

where
\Gamma
is the standard Gamma function
19
. We make the change of variable
y=x/2
in the integral above to get

\forall t\in{[0,\infty[},w(t)=\dfrac{1}{\Gamma(\mathrm{d}/2)}\displaystyle\int_{t/2}^{\infty}y^{\mathrm{d}/2-1}e^{-y}dy=\dfrac{\Gamma(\mathrm{d}/2,t/2)}{\Gamma(\mathrm{d}/2)}\,,

where the second equality derives from
19
. Thus, from
19
, we obtain
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}tw(t)=0
.
∎

7
Clustering Algorithm

We now introduce the algorithm CENTREx that performs clustering over
N
measurement vectors
(\bm{Y}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
, without prior knowledge of the number of clusters. This algorithm works in two stages: it first estimates the cluster centroids, and then assigns each measurement vector to a cluster.
As discussed in Section
4
, the cluster centroids can be estimated by looking for the fixed points of the function
{\bm{g}_{N}}
(see
Equation 8
), given a realization
(\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N})
of
(\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{N})
.

The Mean-Shift algorithm
(
9
)
also estimates the cluster centroids by looking for the fixed-points of
{\bm{g}_{N}}
. To do so, it runs
N
times the iterative procedure
\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}=\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)})
, where each measurement vector
\bm{y}_{n}
serves as an initializer once, by setting
\bm{\varphi}^{(1)}=\bm{y}_{n}
. The Mean-Shift algorithm thus outputs
N
candidate centroids. It then performs an additional step to fuse the candidate centroids that are close enough to each other, thus resulting in a number of centroids near
K
. Although Mean-Shift works well in practice, it becomes very complex when
N
increases. Alternatively, in CENTREx, we rely on the Wald hypothesis test described in Section
6
to eliminate, from the set of potential initializers, all the measurement vectors that are close enough to an already estimated centroid, thus leading to a much less complex algorithm. Since we observed that this method often produces a number of centroids slightly larger than
K
, we still consider the fusion step of Mean-Shift in order to end-up with the correct number of clusters.

In the next subsection, we first describe CENTREx for any given symmetric positive-definite matrices
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!1}
…
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!N}
. Next, in the case of matrices
\bm{C}_{1},\ldots,\bm{C}_{N}
that are equal and proportional to
\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
with unknown proportionality factor
\sigma^{2}
, we propose an MLE
{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\text{MLE}}}
of
\sigma^{2}
in Section
7.2
to tweak CENTREx with
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}={\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}^{2}\,\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
for each
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
. Empirical solutions for estimating unknown matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
, possibly different from each other, are also considered in Section
8.2.6
.

7.1
CENTREx algorithm

The first step of CENTREx is to return an estimate
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}
of the set of centroids
\mathscr{C}
of
(\bm{y}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket}
. To do so, CENTREx initializes
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}
as the empty set
\emptyset
and then estimates the centroids one after the other by relying on the iterative fixed-point computation method described in Section
5.4
. Each time this method is applied, one initializer is picked within the set
\mathcal{U}
of indices of
unmarked
vectors: the marked vectors are all the initializers used so far and all the vectors close enough, in a sense precised below, to previously estimated centroids. Therefore,
\mathcal{U}
is initialized as
\mathcal{U}=\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
. The centroid estimation then comprises three steps (pick, estimate, mark) to be repeated until
\mathcal{U}
is empty. Once the centroid estimation is complete, a fusion algorithm is applied so as to retrieve the correct number of clusters.
Finally, the cluster assignment is performed conventionally by assigning to each measurement the closest estimated centroid. It is completed by removing centroids to which no data have been assigned and have thus produced empty clusters. We outline the algorithm as follows:

Initialization:

\mathcal{U}=\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}=\emptyset

Centroid estimation:

WHILE

\mathcal{U}\neq\emptyset

Step #1:

[Pick]
at random an index
n^{\star}
in the set
\mathcal{U}
.

Step #2:

[Estimate]
a new centroid
\bm{\varphi}
from the fixed-point estimation process described in Section
5.4
, initialized with
\bm{y}^{\star}
. Then update
\widehat{\mathscr{C}}
as
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\leftarrow\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\cup\{\bm{\varphi}\}
.

Step #3:

[Mark]
the measurement vectors that are close enough to
\bm{\varphi}
to consider that they belong to the cluster with centroid
\bm{\varphi}
. Define
\mathcal{V}
as the set of indices of these vectors and update
{\mathcal{U}}
as
\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}\setminus\left({\mathcal{V}}\cup\{n^{\star}\}\right)
.

Fusion:

[Merge]

all centroids
\bm{\varphi},\bm{\varphi}^{\prime}\in\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
such that
\|\bm{\varphi}-\bm{\varphi}^{\prime}\|/\mathrm{d}\leqslant\epsilon_{f}

Cluster assignment:

FOR

n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket

[Assign]

\bm{y}_{n}
to the closest
\bm{\varphi}\in\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}

[Remove] empty clusters

The algorithm does not need prior knowledge of the number
K
of centroids, since its stopping condition bears on the emptiness of
{\mathcal{U}}
. The normalization by
\mathrm{d}
in
[Merge]
to make have
\epsilon_{f}
independent of the data dimension.
We now describe into more details the centroid estimation, the cluster assignment and the fusion. We also introduce additional heuristics.

Remark 3
.

According to
(\textbf{B})
in Theorem
1
, instead of performing the clustering on the basis of
(\bm{y}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket}
, the clustering can alternatively be performed as follows:

1.

Transform the initial data into
\bm{z}_{n}=\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\bm{y}_{n}
for
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,

2.

Calculate
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
by running CENTREx where the input vectors of the centroid estimation are now
(\bm{z}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
and the covariance matrices used to perform the centroid estimation are
{\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}=({\bm{\Delta}^{\prime}}_{\!\!n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
defined according to
Equation 27b
, the empirical parameters
\epsilon_{e}
,
L
and
\alpha
remaining unchanged.

3.

Estimate the centroids by calculating
\bm{R}\xi_{1},\bm{R}\xi_{2},\ldots
where
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots
are the elements of
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
returned by CENTREx.

7.1.1
Centroid estimation

The centroid estimation part of CENTREx is fully stated in Algorithm
1
.
Among the four steps needed to estimate the centroids, the step
[Pick]
is taken as it is: it randomly picks
n^{\star}\in\mathcal{U}
.

To
[Estimate]
, the recursive fixed-point estimation process is initialized with
\bm{\varphi}^{(1)}={\bm{y}_{n^{\star}}}
. We calculate
\bm{\varphi}^{(2)}={\bm{g}_{N}^{(\text{init})}}({\bm{y}_{n^{\star}}})
where
{\bm{g}_{N}^{(\text{init})}}
is
\bm{g}_{N}
parameterized with
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}+{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n^{\star}}
instead of
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
for each
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
. Indeed, for the sequence
\bm{\Phi}
of centroids we want to estimate,
{\bm{y}_{n^{\star}}}
is a realization of
{\bm{Y}_{n^{\star}}}\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\Phi}({\kappa_{n^{\star}}}),\bm{C}_{n^{\star}})
. For any
n\neq n^{\star}
, we thus have
(\bm{Y}_{n}-{\bm{Y}_{n^{\star}}})\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\Phi}(\kappa_{n})-\bm{\Phi}({\kappa_{n^{\star}}}),\bm{C}_{n}+\bm{C}_{n^{\star}})
.

For
\ell\geq 1
, we compute
\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}={\bm{g}_{N}}(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)})
until
\nu_{\bm{Q}}\big(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}-\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)}\big)/\mathrm{d}\leq\epsilon_{e}
or
L
iterations have been performed, where
\nu_{\bm{Q}}
is the Mahalanobis norm associated with the symmetric positive-definite matrix
\bm{Q}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
. The
normalisation by
\mathrm{d}
and the use of
\nu_{\bm{Q}}
in the stopping criterion aim to temper the influence of
\mathrm{d}
and the symmetric positive-definite matrices
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
on the empirical parameter
\epsilon_{e}
. Note that
L
is also an empirical parameter of the algorithm. In addition, the choice of considering
{\bm{g}_{N}^{(\text{init})}}
instead of
\bm{g}_{N}
at the first iteration is a heuristic that was shown to improve the clustering performance in our simulations.

Finally, the
[Mark]
step discards from
\mathcal{U}
the indices of the vectors
\bm{y}_{n}
that are sufficiently close to the newly estimated centroid to consider them as elements of the same cluster. If
{\bm{\varphi}}
denotes this estimated centroid then, for a given realization
\bm{y}_{n}
of
\bm{Y}_{n}\thicksim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\Phi}(\kappa_{n}),\bm{C}_{n})
, the marking amounts to testing the hypothesis that
\bm{\Phi}(\kappa_{n})={\bm{\varphi}}
, which is the condition for
\bm{y}_{n}
to belong to the cluster represented by
\bm{\varphi}
. This testing is performed via the Wald test from
Equation 36
with
\bm{S}={{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
and where
\alpha
is empirically chosen to calculate the threshold value
\mu(\alpha)
according to
Equation 35
. If
\bm{C}_{n}
is known, we set
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=\bm{C}_{n}
and the test is the optimal Wald test to make our decision; otherwise,
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
is an estimate of
\bm{C}_{n}
and we cannot but expect that the use of this estimate in the expression of the Wald test will not affect too much the performance of the test. In this regard, the experimental results of Section
8
illustrate the robustness of CENTREx to various estimates
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
of the matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
.

Algorithm 1

Centroid estimation in CENTREx

(
Centroid_Estim
)

Inputs
:
{\bm{y}}=(\bm{y}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
,
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}=({{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
,
\epsilon_{e}
,
L
,
\alpha

Initialization
:
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\leftarrow\{\emptyset\}
,
\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket

while

\left(\mathcal{U}\neq\{\emptyset\}\right)

do

##
[Pick]
##

Pick randomly an
n^{\star}
in
\mathcal{U}

##
[Estimate]
##

Initialize
\bm{\varphi}^{(1)}=\bm{y}_{n^{\star}}
,
\bm{\varphi}^{(2)}={\bm{g}_{N}^{(\text{init})}}(\bm{\varphi}^{(1)})
,
\ell=2

\bm{Q}\leftarrow\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}

while

\Big(\big(\,\nu_{\bm{Q}}\big(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}-\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)}\big)/\mathrm{d}\geq\epsilon_{e}\,\big)\text{ AND }\big(\ell+1<L\big)\Big)

do

\ell\leftarrow\ell+1

Compute
\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell+1)}=\bm{g}_{N}(\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)})
##
Comment
:
\bm{g}_{N}
is given by
Equation 8
and is parameterized by
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}
and
\bm{y}

end

while

\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\leftarrow\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\cup\left\{\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)}\right\}

##
[Mark]
##

for

n
in
\mathcal{U}

do

if

\big(\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\varphi}^{(\ell)})\leq\mu(\alpha)\big)
or
\big(n=n^{\star}\big)

then

\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}\setminus\{n\}

end

if

end

for

end

while

Outputs
:
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}

7.1.2
Fusion

In our simulations, we observed that the centroid estimation performed by CENTREx may output too many centroids. Therefore, to improve the overall clustering performance, we use a second algorithm, called
fusion
