, to be applied to the output set
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
provided by CENTREx. The fusion algorithm is described in Algorithm
2
. This algorithm first identifies the two centroids
(\bm{\varphi},\bm{\varphi}^{\prime})\!\in\!\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
that are the closest to each other, among all estimated centroids in
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
. It then merges these two centroids if
\|\bm{\varphi}-\bm{\varphi}^{\prime}\|/\mathrm{d}\leqslant\epsilon_{f}
, where the division by
\mathrm{d}
significantly reduces the dependence of
\epsilon_{f}
with the data dimension. The merge is realized by removing
\bm{\varphi}
and
\bm{\varphi}^{\prime}
from
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
, and replacing them with the average centroid
\frac{\bm{\varphi}+\bm{\varphi}^{\prime}}{2}
. The fusion is repeated until the closest two centroids in
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
have distance larger than
\epsilon_{f}
. At the end, the cluster assignment step performed after the last merge provides the output of the clustering algorithm. This fusion step is identical to the additional step for merging the centroids in Mean-Shift.

Algorithm 2

Fusion in CENTREx

(Fusion)

Inputs
:
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
,
\epsilon_{f}

Initialization
:
\Delta=0

while

(
\Delta<\epsilon_{f}
AND
\operatorname{card}(\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}})>1
)

do

Compute
(\bm{\varphi},\bm{\varphi}^{\prime})=\displaystyle\argmin_{(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})\in\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\times\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}},x\neq x^{\prime}}\,\,\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|

\Delta\leftarrow\|\bm{\varphi}-\bm{\varphi}^{\prime}\|/\mathrm{d}

if

{\Delta}<\epsilon_{f}

then

##
[Merge]
##

Update
\hskip 4.26773pt{\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}}\leftarrow\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\setminus\{\bm{\varphi},\bm{\varphi}^{\prime}\}
,
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\leftarrow\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\cup\{\frac{\bm{\varphi}+\bm{\varphi}^{\prime}}{2}\}

end

if

end

while

Outputs
:
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}

7.1.3
Cluster assignment

Once all vectors
\bm{y}_{n}
have been marked, we use the set
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
output by the fusion to perform cluster assignment. The cluster assignment step of CENTREx is fully stated in Algorithm
3
.
In this algorithm, the cluster assignment is realized by selecting for each
\bm{y}_{n}
the closest centroid
\bm{\varphi}\!\in\!\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
.

We could think of carrying the cluster assignment by resorting again to the hypothesis test of
Equation 36
used for the marking operation. However, this test could leave some data with no cluster assignment or, to the contrary, assign some data to several different clusters. This is why here, in order to avoid any underdetermination, we consider the minimum distance condition where we use the Mahalanobis norm
\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}
. After this cluster assignment step, centroids that lead to empty clusters are removed from the set
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
.

Algorithm 3

Cluster assignment in CENTREx

(Cluster_Assignment)

Inputs
:
\bm{y}=(\bm{y}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
,
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}=({{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
,
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}

for

n
in
\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket

do

##
[Assign]
##

Set
\widehat{\bm{\varphi}}_{n}=\displaystyle\arg\min_{\bm{\varphi}\in\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}}\nu_{{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}}(\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{\varphi})

Set
\text{Assign}(n)=\widehat{\bm{\varphi}}_{n}

end

for
##
[Remove] empty clusters
##

for

\bm{\varphi}\in\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}

do

if

\{n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket:\widehat{\bm{\varphi}}_{n}=\bm{\varphi}\}=\emptyset

then

\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\leftarrow\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}\setminus\{\bm{\varphi}\}

end

if

end

for

Outputs
:
\widehat{\hskip 0.00002pt\mathscr{C}}
,
Assign

7.2
The case of proportional covariance matrices

When the symmetric positive-definite matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
are unknown, it is crucial in practice to estimate them with sufficient precision. Unfortunately, (As. 3) encompasses a large class of symmetric positive-definite matrices (diagonal, non-diagonal, heteroscedastic or not, etc.), which makes it difficult to devise a single method to estimate them.

For the special case where
\bm{C}_{n}={\sigma^{2}}\,\bm{C}
for all
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, with known
\bm{C}
and unknown
\sigma
, we propose the following approach to estimate
\sigma
from the sequence of data
(\bm{y}_{n})_{n\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
.
We select
P
different data at random among
(\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N})
, and without loss of generality, re-index them as
(\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{P})
.
We then evaluate the minimum distance
\upsilon
among these
P
data as

\upsilon=\displaystyle\min_{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\text{\scriptsize$(p,p^{\prime})\in\llbracket 1,P\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,P\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket$}\\
\text{\scriptsize$p<p^{\prime}$}\end{array}}\nu^{2}_{\bm{C}}(\bm{y}_{p}-\bm{y}_{p^{\prime}})\,.

(40)

For a given
(p,p^{\prime})\in\llbracket 1,P\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,P\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket
with
p<p^{\prime}
such that
\bm{y}_{p}
and
\bm{y}_{p^{\prime}}
belong to the same cluster,
(\bm{y}_{p}-\bm{y}_{p^{\prime}})
is a realization of
(\bm{Y}_{p}-\bm{Y}_{p^{\prime}})\thicksim\mathcal{N}(0,2{\sigma^{2}}\bm{C})
. In this case, it follows from
Equation 1
and
Equation 2
that
\nu^{2}_{\bm{C}}(\bm{Y}_{p}-\bm{Y}_{p^{\prime}})/2\sigma^{2}\thicksim\chi_{\mathrm{d}}^{2}
, where
\chi_{\mathrm{d}}^{2}
is the Chi-squared distribution with
{\mathrm{d}}
degrees of freedom, and thus, that
\nu^{2}_{\bm{C}}(\bm{Y}_{p}-\bm{Y}_{p^{\prime}})
has cdf
\mathbb{F}
defined by

\forall x\in{[0,\infty[},\mathbb{F}(x)=\mathbb{F}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}(x/2\sigma^{2})\,.

(41)

Equation 40
considers
P(P-1)/2
different pairs
(y_{p},y_{p^{\prime}})
. In what follows, we assume that
M
of these pairs are such that both
y_{p}
and
y_{p^{\prime}}
belong to the same cluster, this cluster being possibly different from one pair to another: given two different pairs
(y_{p},y_{p^{\prime}})
and
(y_{q},y_{q^{\prime}})
among these
M
pairs, both
y_{p}
and
y_{p^{\prime}}
may belong to the same cluster, say cluster
\#k
, whereas both
y_{q}
and
y_{q^{\prime}}
may belong to cluster
\#k^{\prime}
with
k^{\prime}\neq k
. According to this assumption, we consider that
\upsilon
is also the value taken by the minimum
\min(\Upsilon_{1},\ldots,\Upsilon_{M})
of
M
random variables
\Upsilon_{1},\ldots,\Upsilon_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{iid}}}{{\thicksim}}\mathbb{F}
with
M\leqslant P(P-1)/2
.

The cdf of the minimum of a finite sequence of random variables being well-known (see
42
, among others), we calculate the cdf of
\min(\Upsilon_{1},\ldots,\Upsilon_{M})
as

\forall x\in{[0,\infty[},\mathbb{F}_{\min(\Upsilon_{1},\ldots,\Upsilon_{M})}(x)=1-\left(1-\mathbb{F}\left({x}\right)\right)^{M}=1-\left(1-\mathbb{F}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}\left(\frac{x}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\right)^{M}\,.

A pdf of
\min(\Upsilon_{1},\ldots,\Upsilon_{M})
is thus
f_{\min(\Upsilon_{1},\ldots,\Upsilon_{M})}(x)=f(x,\sigma)
for all
x\in{[0,\infty[}
, with

\forall(x,t)\in{[0,\infty[}\times{]0,\infty[},f(x,t)=\textstyle\frac{M}{2{t^{2}}}f_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}(\frac{x}{2{t^{2}}})\left(1-\mathbb{F}_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}\left(\frac{x}{2{t^{2}}}\right)\right)^{M-1}\,,

(42)

where
f_{\chi^{2}_{\mathrm{d}}}
denotes the pdf of the
\chi_{\mathrm{d}}^{2}
distribution. Our MLE
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
is hence defined as

{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}=\argmax_{t\in{]0,\infty[}}f(\upsilon,t)\,.

(43)

Having no closed form for
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
such defined,
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
is calculated numerically. We then set

\forall n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket,{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}={\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\text{MLE}}}\,\bm{C}\,.

(44)

8
Simulations results

In this section, we evaluate the proposed algorithm CENTREx through numerical simulations. We first show clustering results on
2
D toy data sets, and then provide extensive simulation results on synthetic measurement vectors with higher dimension
\mathrm{d}=100
. At the end, we apply CENTREx to the standard data sets Ruspini and IRIS. All simulations described below were performed using MATLAB 2024.

8.1
Two-dimensional toy data sets

Figure 1:
Centre-X applied on the 2D toy data sets provided in the scikit-learn documentation. The red dots are the centroids estimated by CENTREx.

In the scikit-learn documentation
1
1

1

https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/clustering.html
, standard clustering algorithms (K-means, DB-SCAN, Mean-Shift, etc.) are applied to six
2
D toy data sets. We first apply our algorithm CENTREx to these six data sets, in order to check whether the behavior of CENTREx is as expected. We will benchmark our algorithm against other methods later on in this section. We set
L=100
maximum iterations for centroid estimation, and set the empirical parameters as
\epsilon_{e}=10^{-3}
,
\epsilon_{f}=0.5
,
\alpha=10^{-3}
. For each data set, we provide the exact data covariance matrices to our algorithm (their values can be inferred from the provided Python code in the scikit-learn library), in order to observe the performance of CENTREx in its best configuration. In the same way, all clustering algorithms considered in the scikit-learn evaluation
1
1
footnotemark:

1

were provided with correct input parameters (for instance, K-means was given the correct value of
K
, etc.).

The clustering results obtained by CENTREx on the six data sets are shown in Figure
1
. On the three data sets that consider Gaussian data (top right, bottom left, bottom middle), we observe that CENTREx was able to correctly retrieve
K=3
clusters of correct shapes. This result was expected, since these three data sets follow the Gaussian model of CENTREx. On the opposite, CENTREx was not able to correctly cluster two of the other data sets (top left, top middle) since these data sets do not follow the Gaussian model. Finally, CENTREx retrieved one cluster on the last data set (bottom right), for which there is no obvious clustering solution.

8.2
Measurement vectors with dimension
\mathrm{d}=100

We now extensively evaluate the performance of CENTREx on synthetic data sets with higher dimension
{\mathrm{d}}=100
. Simulations on synthetic data are useful to evaluate the performance and robustness of CENTREx, depending on whether the symmetric positive-definite matrices
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
equal the true covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
, or not. They also permit to assess the relevance of the proposed Wald kernel.

8.2.1
Data generation

At the start of each experiment, we choose an interval
[\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}}]\subset{]0,\infty[}
to generate the measurements. Given
[\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}}]
, we generate
800
data sets. Each of these data sets is obtained as follows.

First, the number
K
of centroids is randomly drawn in
\llbracket 2,10\rrbracket
according to the discrete uniform law on this interval. Second, once
K
is determined, we randomly generate
K
centroids in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
by drawing
K
realizations of a centred
\mathrm{d}
-dimensional Gaussian random vector with covariance matrix
A^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
with
A=20
, until the minimum distance between two different centroids exceeds
\mu_{\min}=200
. The choice of the maximum number of centroids, that of the value of
\mu_{\min}
and that of
A
, are arbitrary. Finally, when the
K
centroids have been calculated, we generate
N={400}
realizations
\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N}
of measurement vectors
\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{N}
. Each of these measurement vectors has mean randomly chosen among the
K
available centroids, via the discrete uniform law on
\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, and satisfies the model of Section
5.1.1
and
3
, with
\bm{R}=\bm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}
. This is equivalent to considering the case where
\bm{R}=\bm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}
in
Equation 27
or, in the vein of Remarks
2
and
3
, to considering that data are issued from the transform
\overline{\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}}
, which consists of multiplying the initial measurement vectors by
\bm{R}^{\mathrm{T}}
.

8.2.2
Clustering algorithms and brief outline of the simulations

Beside CENTREx, we systematically evaluate the K-means algorithm in our experiments, since this algorithm is widely used for clustering. To mitigate initialization issues with K-means, we actually use the K-means++ algorithm with
10
random initializations. In addition, for K-means, we consider both the setup where
K
is known and the one where
K
is unknown. The latter case is referred to as the X-means algorithm, and consists in relaunching K-means with all possible values of
K
in a certain interval, and select the
K
that maximizes the Silhouette coefficient
(
45
)
. X-means will search
K
in the interval
\llbracket 1,10\rrbracket
.

We will also assess the performance of Mean-Shift with the standard Gaussian kernel, the flat kernel, and the proposed Wald kernel. The Mean-Shift code in the scikit-learn library is implemented with the flat kernel only. We thus use this library to perform Mean-Shift with flat kernel. To perform Mean-Shift with either the Gaussian or the Wald kernel, we implemented the Mean-Shift algorithm as follows. According to
Equation 9
, we particularized the mean-shift function of CENTREx to the
mean-shift
of
9
; next, according to Section
7.1
, Mean-Shift amounts to performing
[Estimate]
for each of the
N
input data and, skipping the marking, directly merging the candidate centroids obtained after these
N
runs. For the fusion step in Mean-Shift, we use
\epsilon_{f}=1
, as will be considered for CENTREx.

Several empirical parameters must be provided to CENTREx. We set
\alpha=10^{-3}
since we observed that, most often, any value of
\alpha
equal or lower than
10^{-2}
leads to the same clustering performance. In the same way, the parameters
\epsilon_{e}
and
L
do not seem to influence much the clustering performance, and we set
\epsilon_{e}=10^{-3}
and
L=100
. On the other hand, we observed that the fusion parameter
\epsilon_{f}
has a greater impact on the clustering performance. Given that it is used for the fusion step, we propose to set
\epsilon_{f}=\mu_{\text{min}}/2\mathrm{d}
, since
\mu_{\text{min}}/2
is the distance at which a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{d}}
cannot unambiguously be assigned to any two centroids. Therefore, for
\mathrm{d}=100
and
\mu_{\text{min}}=200
, we set
\epsilon_{f}=1
.

As an introduction to their detailed descriptions below, we briefly outline our simulations. In our first experiment (see Figure
2
), we consider the homoscedastic case of matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
all equal to
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}
. Because of their similarities, we compare CENTREx to Mean-Shift when both employ the same kernel, either the Gaussian kernel or the Wald kernel, and both are provided with the value of
\sigma
. In Figures
3
,
4
and
5
, we benchmark CENTREx with K-means++ and X-means to evaluate the robustness of these three algorithms. In Figure
3
, we consider the homoscedastic case of matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
all equal to
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}
, where
\sigma
is now unknown and estimated by the MLE of Section
7.2
. In Figures
4
and
5
, we address two different scenarios for generating heteroscedastic diagonal matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
that are not identity-scaled. The purpose is to assess the performance of CENTREx tweaked with either the exact knowledge of these matrices or different estimates of them.

8.2.3
Performance evaluation criteria

In the figures displayed below, the four criteria used to evaluate the performance of each benchmarked algorithm are: 1) the proportion of correctly estimated values of
K
, 2) the Silhouette coefficient, 3) the clustering error rate, 4) the average estimated value of
K
.

The clustering error rate is defined as follows. Given
N
data
\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N}
, we define the upper-triangular adjacency matrix as

\bm{A}_{\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N}}=(a_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N,1\leqslant j\leqslant N}

where, for
{(i,j)\in\llbracket 1,N-1\rrbracket\times\llbracket i+1,N\rrbracket}
,
a_{i,j}=1
if
\bm{y}_{i}
and
\bm{y}_{j}
belong to the same cluster and
a_{i,j}=0
otherwise. Similarly, after clustering by a given algorithm, we can define the output upper-triangular adjacency matrix

\widehat{\bm{A}}_{\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N}}=(\widehat{a}_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N,1\leqslant j\leqslant N}

where, for
{(i,j)\in\llbracket 1,N-1\rrbracket\times\llbracket i+1,N\rrbracket}
,
\widehat{a}_{i,j}=1
if
\bm{y}_{i}
and
\bm{y}_{j}
are assigned to the same cluster and
\widehat{a}_{i,j}=0
otherwise. We define the clustering error rate of the algorithm under consideration by the Hamming distance between
\bm{A}_{\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N}}
and
\widehat{\bm{A}}_{\bm{y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{y}_{N}}
divided by
N(N-1)/2
. In other words, the Error Rate induced by the given algorithm is calculated as

\text{Error Rate}=\frac{2}{N(N-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\sum_{j=i+1}^{N}|\widehat{a}_{i,j}-a_{i,j}|\,.

(45)

8.2.4
Clustering performance for constant known scaled-identity covariance matrices (see Figure
2
)

We consider the case of covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
all equal to
\sigma^{2}\,\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
, where
\sigma>0
is known. We thus take
\sigma_{\text{min}}=\sigma_{\text{max}}=\sigma
. In our simulations,
\sigma\in[1,40]
. Our benchmarking involves:

•

CENTREx (Wald,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) and CENTREx (Gauss,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
): CENTREx with Wald kernel and CENTREx with Gaussian kernel, both with
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=\sigma^{2}\,\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
for each
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
; the Gaussian kernel used is
w_{\text{Gauss}}(t)=e^{-{t^{2}}/{2c\sigma^{2}}}(t\geqslant 0),
where
c
is an empirical coefficient chosen equal to
5
after preliminary trials;

•

Mean-Shift (Wald), Mean-Shift (Gauss) and Mean-Shift (flat): Mean-Shift with Wald kernel, Gaussian kernel and flat kernel, respectively; for each variant,
h=\sigma
in
Equation 9
and the Gaussian kernel is
w_{\text{Gauss}}
defined above;

•

K-means++ and X-means.

We observe in Figure
2
that Mean-Shift (Wald) and CENTREx (Wald,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) on the one hand, and Mean-Shift (Gauss) and CENTREx (Gauss,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) on the other hand, yield very close results. We also notice that CENTREx (Gauss,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) slightly outperforms Mean-Shift (Gauss). As expected, Mean-Shift (flat) yields the worst performance measurements, even for small values of
\sigma
, noticeably. That Mean-Shift and CENTREx yield similar performance measurements, when both are equipped with the same kernel, follows from the next remark.

Remark 4
.

Regardless of the covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
for
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, when
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=h^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
for some real value
h
and for all
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, the mean-shift function
\bm{g}_{N}
defined by
Equation 8
reduces to the mean-shift of
Equation 9
used in
9
. According to their description above, the sole difference between CENTREx and the Mean-Shift algorithm is the marking step performed by CENTREx to reduce the number of estimated fixed-points to merge. Therefore, when
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=h^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
for some real value
h
and all
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, and regardless of the covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}
for
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, CENTREx and the Mean-Shift algorithm, whether they both use the Wald kernel or the Gaussian kernel, will perform similarly, the key advantage of CENTREx compared to Mean-Shift being that CENTREx is much less complex than Mean-Shift, as already pointed out in Section
7
.

Figure
2
shows also that the Wald kernel noticeably improves the clustering performance, since the performance measurements of Mean-Shift (Wald) and CENTREx (Wald,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) are significantly higher than those of Mean-Shift (Gauss) and CENTREx (Gauss,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
).

In terms of proportion of correct
K
, CENTREx (Wald,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) and Mean-Shift (Wald) outperform X-means for
\sigma\leqslant 30
. For
\sigma>30
, the proportion of correct
K
of CENTREx (Wald,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) and Mean-Shift (Wald) drops, whereas X-means maintains a proportion of correct
K
above
0.9
. Note also that the error rates of K-means++ and X-means are larger than those of CENTREx (Wald,
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) and Mean-Shift (Wald) for
\sigma
above
7
. This trend will be verified in our next experimental results.

We must also notice the significant hump of the Silhouette curve obtained for Mean-Shift (flat). This is not contradictory to the performance of this algorithm in terms of proportion of correct
K
and error rate. Indeed, Figure
2
(d) presents the average estimated value of
K
in function of
\sigma
for all the benchmarked algorithms. Since the value of
K
is distributed uniformly between
2
and
10
, the empirical average value of
K
should be
6
. Mean-Shift (flat) achieves this empirical value for
\sigma\leqslant 20
. However, for
\sigma>20
, Mean-Shift (flat) overestimates
K
, before underestimating it for
\sigma
above
30
.

According to the foregoing, we will now always equip CENTREx and the Mean-Shift algorithm with the Wald kernel. The kernel will thus not be mentioned any longer in the legend of the next figures.

8.2.5
Clustering performance for constant unknown scaled-identity covariance matrices (see Figure
3
)

We still consider the homoscedastic case of equal covariance matrices
\bm{C}_{n}=\sigma^{2}\,\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
for all
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
. We thus take
\sigma=\sigma_{\text{min}}=\sigma_{\text{max}}
again. This time, we consider Mean-Shift and CENTREx, both with the Wald kernel, when
\sigma
is estimated by
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
calculated according to Subsection
7.2
, with
M=P=50
in
Equation 42
and

\bm{C}=\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
in
Equation 44
.

To further assess their performance and robustness, we present in Figure
3
the clustering performance of:

•

CENTREx (
{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\text{MLE}}}\,\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) with, for any
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}={\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\text{MLE}}}\,\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
;

•

Mean-Shift (
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
) where we set
h={\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
in
Equation 9
;

•

CENTREx (
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) already considered in the previous experiment.

•

K-means++ and X-means.

CENTREx (
{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\text{MLE}}}\,\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) and Mean-Shift (
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
) perform similarly as explained by Remark
4
above. CENTREx (
{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\text{MLE}}}\,\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) suffers from a limited performance degradation compared to CENTREx (
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
). The behavior of CENTREx (
{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\text{MLE}}}\,\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) in comparison with X-means and K-means++ is thus similar to that of CENTREx (
\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
). This means that if we have a constant standard deviation for the data, CENTREx can be used even if this standard deviation is unknown and estimated.

8.2.6
Clustering performance for varying diagonal covariance matrices (See Figures
4
and
5
)

In this section, for each
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, we assume a diagonal covariance matrix

\bm{C}_{n}=\bm{\Delta}_{n}=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma^{2}_{n,1},\ldots,\sigma^{2}_{n,\mathrm{d}})

where, for each
i\in\llbracket 1,\mathrm{d}\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket
,
\sigma_{n,i}
is randomly chosen in the interval
[\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}}]
, with
\sigma_{\text{max}}=\sigma_{\text{min}}+4
and
\sigma_{\text{min}}
ranging in
[1,40]
. The probability distribution followed by the values
\sigma_{n,i}
will be specified below and will be different for Figure
4
and Figure
5
.

When the exact values of the coefficients
\sigma_{n,i}
are unknown to CENTREx, it would be desirable to estimate them. Unfortunately, we have no theoretical answer to this estimation problem. We thus proceed empirically instead, by setting
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
for all
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, where
\widehat{\sigma}\in{]0,\infty[}
is chosen as a unique estimate for all the unknown standard deviations
\sigma_{n,i}
. In this respect, the experimental results of Figures
4
and
5
below are obtained by using the following values for
\widehat{\sigma}
:

(i)
\widehat{\sigma}=\sigma_{\text{min}}
;

(ii)
\widehat{\sigma}=\sigma_{\text{max}}
;

(iii)
\widehat{\sigma}=\sigma_{\text{mean}}=(\sigma_{\text{min}}+\sigma_{\text{max}})/2
;

(iv)
\widehat{\sigma}={\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
, where
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
is calculated according to Subsection
7.2
with same parameter values as those used to produce Figure
3
.

The difference between Figures
4
and
5
lies in the way we generate data.

To obtain Figure
4
, data are generated according to Section
8.2.1
with, for any
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
and any
i\in\llbracket 1,\mathrm{d}\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket
,
\sigma_{n,i}
randomly drawn with uniform distribution in
[\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}}]
. In Figure
4
, we then display the results achieved by:

•

CENTREx with the different setups (i), (ii), (iii) and (iv) proposed above and respectively denoted by CENTREx (
\sigma_{\text{min}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
), CENTREx (
\sigma_{\text{max}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
), CENTREx (
\sigma_{\text{mean}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) and CENTREx (
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
);

•

CENTREx (
\Delta
), that is, CENTREx with perfect knowledge of the matrices
\bm{\Delta}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\Delta}_{N}
;

•

K-means++ and X-means.

The Mean-Shift algorithm is not considered because, according to Remark
4
, Mean-Shift equipped with the Wald kernel and
\widehat{\sigma}\in\{\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}},\sigma_{\text{mean}},{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}\}
would yield approximately the same results as CENTREx (
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
).

We first observe that CENTREx (
\sigma_{\text{mean}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) yields no significant performance loss with respect to CENTREx (
\Delta
). Therefore, setting
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=\sigma_{\text{mean}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
for all
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
can be seen as a satisfactory solution in the experimental setting considered here. This is not surprising since
\sigma_{\text{mean}}
is the expectation of the uniform distribution according to which the standard deviations
\sigma_{n,i}
are chosen in
[\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}}]
for all
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
and all
i\in\llbracket 1,\mathrm{d}\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket
.

Although the theoretical hypotheses of Section
7.2
to compute
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
are clearly not satisfied under the present experimental setting, CENTREx (
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) undergoes only a slight performance loss in comparison to Fig.
3
. The clusters generated by choosing the standard deviations
\sigma_{n,i}
uniformly and independently in the same interval
[\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}}]
are likely not heteroscedastic enough for CENTREx (
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) to achieve performance measurements significantly different from those of Fig.
3
.

It must also be noticed that CENTREx (
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) and CENTREx (
\sigma_{\text{max}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) perform similarly. In contrast, CENTREx (
\sigma_{\text{min}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) performs very poorly, with a significant overestimation of the number of centroids. In the present experimental setting, overestimating noise standard deviations is far better than underestimating them.

Clustering performance of CENTREx vs. K-means++ and X-means. For each
\sigma_{\text{min}}\!\in\![1,40]
and for all
n\!\in\!\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
\bm{C}_{n}
is a diagonal matrix
\bm{\Delta}_{n}
with random diagonal elements uniformly distributed in
[\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}}]
with
\sigma_{\text{max}}=\sigma_{\text{min}}+4
. CENTREx (
\bm{\Delta}
) is CENTREx with perfect knowledge of
\bm{\Delta}_{1},\ldots,\bm{\Delta}_{N}
and, for different
\widehat{\sigma}
, CENTREx (
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) is CENTREx with
\bm{\Delta}_{n}=\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
for all
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
: (a) Percentage of correctly retrieved number of clusters; (b) Silhouette; (c) Error rate; (d) Average estimated value of
K
; (e) zoom on error rate.

The heteroscedastic clusters generated to obtain Figure
4
does not make it possible to exhibit clear differences in performance between CENTREx (
\Delta
) and CENTREx (
\sigma_{\text{mean}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
), on the one hand, and between CENTREx (
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
) and CENTREx (
\sigma_{\text{max}}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
), on the other hand. Thence the following experimental setting to make appear such differences in our next Figure
5
.

Given the intervals
I_{0}=[\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{min}}+1]
and
I_{1}=[\sigma_{\text{max}}-1,\sigma_{\text{max}}]
, the results of Figure
5
are obtained by generating data as follows.

1.

Compute the number
K
of centroids and the centroids themselves as described in Section
8.2.1
.

2.

For each
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, choose the mean of the measurement vector
\bm{Y}_{n}
among the
K
available centroids, as specified in Section
8.2.1
.

3.

For each
k\in\llbracket 1,K\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
, perform an independent and equiprobable Bernoulli trial
B_{k}
valued in
\{0,1\}
. For all
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
such that
\bm{Y}_{n}
belongs to cluster
\#k
, set

J_{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\!I_{0}&\text{if $B_{k}=0$}\\
\!I_{1}&\text{otherwise.~}\end{array}\right.

4.

Compute the covariance matrix of each measurement vector
\bm{Y}_{n}
belonging to the
k^{\text{th}}
cluster as
\bm{C}_{n}=\bm{\Delta}_{n}=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma^{2}_{n,1},\ldots,\sigma^{2}_{n,\mathrm{d}}),
where the standard deviations
\sigma_{n,i}
are randomly and independently drawn with uniform distribution in the interval
J_{n}
.

We thus create heteroscedastic clusters that are either small, in that the standard deviations of the coordinates of their elements are all in
I_{0}
, or large, in that the standard deviations of the coordinates of their elements are all in
I_{1}
.

We then perform the clustering of the data via CENTREx, equipped with the Wald kernel, but tested with different choices for the matrices
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}
when
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
. In our benchmarking, we take into account that the intervals
J_{1},\ldots,J_{N}
basically provide a more precise location of the standard deviations
\sigma_{n,i}
than
[\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}}]
, and that these intervals can be assumed to be known regardless of the Bernoulli trials from which they are generated.

Specifically, in Figure
5
, we assess:

•

CENTREx (
\bm{\Delta}
) with, for any
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=\bm{\Delta}_{n}
;

•

CENTREx (
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}
) with, for any
n\in\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}=\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}_{n}=\operatorname{diag}(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{n,1},\ldots,\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{n,\mathrm{d}})
where, for any
i\in\llbracket 1,\mathrm{d}\hskip 0.28436pt\rrbracket
,
\widehat{\sigma}_{n,i}
is the middle point of the interval
J_{n}
in which the value
{\sigma}_{n,i}
has been drawn;

•

CENTREx (
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
) with, for any
n\!\in\!\llbracket 1,N\hskip 0.42677pt\rrbracket
,
{{\bm{C}^{\prime}}}_{\!\!n}\!=\!\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\!\bm{I}_{{\mathrm{d}}}
and
\widehat{\sigma}\!\in\!\{{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}},\sigma_{\text{mean}},\sigma_{\text{min}},\sigma_{\text{max}}\}
as in Figure
4
;

•

K-means++ and X-means.

We observe that CENTREx (
\widehat{\bm{\Delta}}
) achieves the same performance as CENTREx (
{\bm{\Delta}}
). This is explained by the fact that both
I_{0}
and
I_{1}
are sufficiently small for their middle point to be good enough estimates of random variables uniformly distributed over each of them. Due to the heteroscedasticity of the measurement vectors, the other variants of CENTREx undergo a performance loss compared to the previous figures. Again, the hypotheses underlying the computation of
{\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{MLE}}}
in Section
7.2
are clearly not satisfied under the present experimental setting where the measurement vectors used to generate the data are heteroscedastic.

k
, we equiprobably choose

. The covariance matrix of each

in cluster #

is a diagonal matrix
